28 October 2026

Economic and fiscal outlook – October 2026

The latest update of our forecasts was published on 28 October 2026 in the October 2026 Economic and fiscal outlook. Read the Executive summary for the key messages of our forecast or the full report on our website.

pdf
Economic and fiscal outlook – October 2026
28 October 2026 – 1.69 MB

Chapter 2 sets out our forecasts for the economy over a five-year horizon. We cover our latest forecast changes in light of recent developments and the effect of policies in the Autumn Budget.

In Chapter 3, details the policy measures announced since March 2025, provides an update on selected previous measures, and discusses policy risks and uncertainties.

Chapters 4-6 sets out our forecasts for receipts and public spending over a five-year horizon. We also explain our loans and other financial transactions forecasts. All this, together with new policy decisions, builds the outlook for borrowing and debt.

In Chapter 7, we assess the Government against its fiscal targets and assesses their likelihood of being met on current policy under our central forecast. We consider the uncertainty around our economic and fiscal forecasts and the risks to the Government meeting its targets. We also test the sensitivity of our fiscal forecasts in an alternative economic scenario.

Annex A contains detailed summary tables setting out our economic and fiscal forecasts.

At a glance

Foreword

This Economic and fiscal outlook (EFO) sets out our central forecast, alongside the risks and uncertainties that surround it, for the five years to 2031-32. It takes account of recent data and other developments, alongside government policies announced since our last forecast – up to and including the October 2026 Budget. The forecasts presented in this document represent our view as the three independent members of the OBR’s Budget Responsibility Committee (BRC). We take full responsibility for the judgements that underpin them and for the conclusions we have reached.

As always, we have been greatly supported in our work by the staff of the OBR. We are very grateful for their hard work and expertise. We have also drawn on the work and expertise of officials across government in preparing these forecasts. We are grateful for their engagement and insight.

We present the economy forecast in calendar years and produce quarterly paths. The fiscal forecast is typically presented on a financial year basis which runs from April to March. For the current fiscal year, 2026-27, outturn data is incomplete and is, therefore, presented as an OBR forecast. Our online detailed forecast tables, and charts and tables data files, detail the points where individual forecast variables are outturn and forecasts.

On 20 July, we provided an assessment of the outlook for the economy and public finances to the incoming Chancellor of the Exchequer, to support his initial preparations ahead of the Budget. This assessment was subsequently used for the initial round of our pre-measures forecast. The date for the Budget was then announced on 31 July, which provided more than the ten weeks’ notice required by the Memorandum of understanding between the Office for Budget Responsibility, HM Treasury, the Department for Work and Pensions and HM Revenue and Customs (MoU). After this, the timetable for the production of the forecast proceeded as follows:

  • Over the remainder of the summer, the OBR developed the pre-measures economic forecast, drawing on new data releases and other information available during this period. At various points, using the economic determinants from these forecasts (such as the components of nominal income and spending, unemployment, inflation, and interest rates), we commissioned updated forecasts from the relevant government departments for the various tax and spending items that in aggregate determine the position of the public finances. We discussed these in detail with the officials producing them, which allowed us to investigate proposed changes in forecasting methodology and to assess the significance of recent tax and spending outturn data.
  • The pre-measures economy forecast was completed on 8 September and was based on financial and energy market data averaged over the 10 working days to 19 August. This is a longer gap between the closure of the pre-measures economy forecast and publication of the final forecast than is set out within the MoU, and was requested by the Treasury to allow for greater policy decision-making time after receiving the final pre-measures forecast. We agreed to this change on the basis that it would also provide the OBR with greater time to effectively scrutinise the economic and fiscal impact of policy decisions, and to finalise and quality assure our analysis. We will review the effectiveness of this change with the Treasury ahead of our next forecast.
  • Once completed, the pre-measures economy forecast was sent to government departments to produce individual tax and spending forecasts and they returned them on the same day. These fed into a final pre-measures fiscal forecast that was completed on 18 September. The fiscal forecast is consistent with the ONS Public sector finances (PSF) release on 21 August, including ONS estimates of revisions that would arise in the 22 September release, as well as management information on tax receipts for August. In line with recent practice, we took a later window for interest rates in the fiscal forecast, over the five working days to 15 September. This results in very minor discrepancies between the economy and fiscal forecast which we judge to be immaterial.
  • The final pre-measures economic and fiscal forecast was sent to the Chancellor on 18 September. At the request of the Treasury, this was the only pre-measures economic and fiscal forecast round that was shared with them during this forecast. This represented a change from the process followed in recent years, when we shared interim rounds of the pre-measures economic and fiscal forecasts with the Treasury as they were completed. No further material adjustments were made to our economy or fiscal forecasts after this, other than to take account of the impact of policy measures provided by the Treasury. We always reserve the right to reopen our pre-measures forecasts in the event of exceptional circumstances which lead to a significant and structural change in the economic and fiscal outlook. We did not judge that to be the case at this event.
  • In parallel to the production of the forecast, we scrutinised the costing of a number of individual tax and spending measures announced since the March 2026 forecast. As usual, OBR staff and the BRC requested further information and/or changes to many of the draft costings prepared by HMRC, DWP and other departments.
  • We made an initial assessment of the economic and fiscal effects of the emerging Budget policy package that was provided by the Treasury on 25 September. We incorporated this package into an initial post-measures forecast, to provide an early view on the effect of policy measures on the economy and public finances, which we sent to the Chancellor on 2 October. This forecast round was produced using our internal ready-reckoner models rather than being sent to departmental forecasters.
  • In line with the agreed timetable, on 9 October the Treasury provided the final package of measures that could cause movements in our economy forecast. We sent the resulting final economy forecast to the Treasury on 16 October and a near-final fiscal forecast on 20 October. Final policy decisions, that would only affect the fiscal forecast, were returned by the Treasury on 20 October, and our forecast was then finalised and sent to the Treasury on 22 October.

The Treasury made a written request, as provided for in the MoU between us, that we provide the Chancellor and an agreed list of special advisers and officials with a near-final draft of the EFO on 22 October. This allowed the Treasury to prepare the Chancellor’s statement and accompanying documents. We also provided pre-release access to the full and final EFO on 26 October.

The agreed forecast timetable was largely adhered to. Small changes were made to the parameters of one policy measure following the costings certification deadline, meaning that, although we have no concerns about the methodology used, we have not been able to certify this costing. We have used the Government’s estimates of its fiscal impact and will return to it at the next forecast. In addition, two policy measures with direct impacts on our economy forecast were altered after it had been finalised. Had we been able to incorporate these, they would have only made a small difference to our final economy forecast.

During the forecasting period, the BRC held dozens of scrutiny and challenge meetings with officials from other departments, in addition to numerous further meetings at staff level and with external stakeholders. We have been provided with all the information and analysis that we requested and have come under no pressure from Ministers, advisers, or officials to change any of our conclusions. A full log of our substantive contact with Ministers, their offices, and special advisers since we published our previous forecast in March 2026 can be found on our website. This includes the list of special advisers and officials who received the near-final draft of the EFO on 22 October.

In this EFO, we present a detailed central forecast in line with the responsibility in our primary legislation to assess the extent to which the fiscal mandate has been, or is likely to be, achieved. The fiscal mandate is currently defined as a target in the third year of our forecast. This requires us to produce a precise forecast for a set of very uncertain outcomes. For example, the borrowing forecast represents the difference between over £1.5 trillion each of receipts and expenditure by the end of the forecast period. We therefore also present a range of different indicators of uncertainty to illustrate the potential effects of alternative scenarios, sensitivities and risks that inevitably lie around the central forecast. For fiscal policy-makers, and those who scrutinise fiscal policy, we see this analysis as being as important as the central forecast. The central forecast remains our best estimate of the central outlook for the economy and public finances, within the constraints in which we are required to make it.

We would be pleased to receive feedback on any aspect of the content or presentation of our analysis. This can be sent to [email protected].

The Budget Responsibility Committee
Professor Jonathan Haskel CBE, Professor David Miles CBE and Tom Josephs

Chapter 1: Executive summary

Overview

1.1 The UK’s economic and fiscal position remains very challenging. Since 2008, repeated adverse shocks have contributed to persistently weak GDP growth and elevated levels of government borrowing. This has resulted in a sharp increase in government debt as a share of GDP, to a level that is high by both recent historical and international standards.

1.2 There have been further adverse developments since our March forecast, with both interest rates and energy prices rising significantly. As a net importer of energy, the rise in energy prices constitutes a negative terms-of-trade shock for the UK, which pushes up on inflation and lowers real household disposable incomes. In the central forecast – the mid-point of a wide range of possible outcomes – CPI inflation is 3½ per cent in 2027, compared to 2 per cent expected in March. Real GDP growth slows from around 1¼ per cent in 2026 to 1 per cent in 2027 – the latter around a ½ percentage point lower than the March forecast. Assuming energy prices subsequently decline in line with market pricing, inflation returns to 2 per cent by 2028 and GDP returns to the level expected in March by 2030. After falling slightly in the near term, per person real household disposable income in 2030-31 is over ¾ per cent below our March forecast and only 2 per cent above its pre-pandemic level.

1.3 The rise in interest rates and increase in the inflation forecast leaves pre-measures borrowing an average of £11 billion per year higher than in March. The direct effects of policy measures further increase borrowing by £8 billion in 2026-27, £4 billion in 2027-28, and £0.4 billion in 2028-29, and then reduce borrowing by £7 billion, on average, in the years thereafter. Overall, while borrowing as a share of GDP is still forecast to decline over the forecast to just under 2 per cent of GDP, these changes slow the near-term pace of consolidation compared to previous plans. Public sector net debt peaks around 97 per cent of GDP in 2028-29, before falling slightly to 96 per cent in 2031-32. In the central forecast, the fiscal mandate for the current budget to be in surplus in 2029-30 is met by a margin of £15 billion. The supplementary target for public sector net financial liabilities (PSNFL) to be falling as a share of GDP in 2029-30 is met by a margin of £24 billion.

1.4 The outlook for the economy and public finances is uncertain, with significantly better or worse outcomes of broadly equal probability. If energy prices reach a higher and more sustained peak, borrowing could be £17 billion above the central forecast in 2027-28, while a lower energy price path could reduce borrowing by £12 billion. A one per cent change in interest rates could raise or reduce government debt interest costs by £16 billion. If productivity growth were 0.5 percentage points higher or lower than forecast, borrowing could reach £50 billion lower or £40 billion higher. Significant pressures on departmental spending include higher inflation, public sector pay, and policy ambitions such as defence.

Economic and fiscal context

1.5 Adverse shocks have continued to hit the UK since the 2008 global financial crisis, including rising barriers to trade after EU exit, the pandemic, and the 2022 energy price spike. These shocks, alongside other structural trends, have resulted in weak growth in GDP per person, periods of elevated inflation, and low growth in real household disposable incomes. On top of these, since we finalised our March forecast, there has been another spike in energy prices and a significant rise in interest rates. Gilt yields have returned closer to their long-run averages following a period of exceptionally low rates after the financial crisis.

1.6 These adverse shocks and low economic growth have resulted in elevated levels of government borrowing and debt in the UK. Chart 1.1 shows that UK borrowing has followed a similar trend to the G7 and advanced economy average over the past two decades but at a consistently higher level. As a result, UK government debt as a share of GDP has increased by more than in any other G7 economy, rising from 38 per cent in 2006 to reach 93 per cent in 2026. The cost of servicing this debt has also increased sharply recently – from 1.7 per cent of GDP (£39 billion) in 2019-20 to 3.5 per cent of GDP (£109 billion) in 2025-26. In the IMF’s forecasts, UK borrowing falls to close to the advanced economy average by 2031, which would lead to debt stabilising at around the G7 average.

Chart 1.1: General government borrowing and net debt in advanced economies

Note: This chart shows IMF outturn (2006-2025) and forecast (2026-2031) for general government net borrowing and net debt which excludes the activity of public corporations. The lines show borrowing and debt for the UK, the unweighted G7 average excluding the UK and the unweighted advanced economy average excluding the UK. The advanced economies included in the average are those detailed in the IMF World economic outlook. The swathe shows the range of borrowing and debt for G7 economies excluding the UK. 

Source: IMF, OBR

Economic outlook

1.7 Energy prices have increased sharply since our last forecast in March. The left panel of Chart 1.2 shows that market pricing, at the time that our forecast was conditioned, implied that oil prices would fall from their peak of 95 dollars a barrel in the second quarter of this year to just above 70 dollars a barrel by late 2028 and remain around that level thereafter. The right panel shows that gas prices were expected to peak at around 150 pence a therm at the end of this year before falling back to around 70 pence a therm from mid-2028. Compared with the March forecast, market pricing for oil and gas is significantly higher in the near term, by 30 and 80 per cent respectively, but in our central forecast they are only around 10 per cent higher in 2030. Oil and gas futures have increased further since we took market expectations – at the time of finalising this report, they were close to the top of the range since the beginning of the Middle East conflict. Given the uncertainty around the outlook for energy prices, we have produced scenarios assessing the potential economic and fiscal implications of alternative paths for these in Chapter 7.

Chart 1.2: Energy prices

Note: October 2026 forecast uses the 10 working days to 19 August and March 2026 forecast uses the 10 working days to 22 January. Range since Middle East conflict is range between 2 March and 20 October.

Source: Bloomberg, Ofgem, OBR

1.8 The future market-implied path of Bank Rate and gilt yields has also increased significantly since our March forecast (Chart 1.3). Our central economy forecast is conditioned on Bank Rate reaching just over 4¼ per cent by mid-2027 and just over 4½ per cent by the end of the forecast period, around ¾ and ½ a percentage point higher than our March forecast, respectively. In our fiscal forecast, which was finalised later than the economy forecast, Bank Rate is a further ¼ percentage point higher on average. Gilt yields have risen substantially across all maturities since our March forecast.

Chart 1.3: Bank Rate and gilt yields

Note: October 2026 economy forecast uses 10 working days to 19 August, October 2026 fiscal forecast uses 5 working days to 15 September, and March 2026 forecast uses the 10 working days to 22 January. Range since Middle East conflict is range between 2 March and 20 October.

Source: Bank of England, OBR

1.9 As Chart 1.4 shows, in our central forecast, higher energy prices contribute to CPI inflation increasing to a quarterly peak of 3¾ per cent in early 2027. It is then forecast to fall to 2 per cent in 2028 as energy prices drop back. Inflation is about ¾ and 1½ percentage points higher in 2026 and 2027, respectively, than the March forecast and returns to target around one year later. This reflects the direct effect of higher energy prices on fuel prices and utilities, the indirect effect of energy prices as an input into the cost of other products, and some second-round effects of higher inflation through wage growth. But we judge that these second-round effects will be less inflationary than those following the previous energy price spike in 2022, as a significantly looser labour market restricts workers’ capacity to bargain for higher wages. This means that nominal wage growth in the central forecast is only around ½ and ¾ percentage points higher in 2026 and 2027, respectively, than in March.

1.10 The outlook for inflation is highly uncertain, including the extent to which energy prices generate second–round effects through wages. This is important for the fiscal forecast given the high effective tax rate on labour income and frozen income tax thresholds. Based purely on past forecast errors, Chart 1.4 suggests that there is a one-in-five chance that CPI inflation in 2027 will be above 4½ per cent and a similar probability that it will be below 2½ per cent.

Chart 1.4: CPI inflation

Note: The chart shows annual average CPI inflation. The forecast starts from 2026. The fan chart uses past forecast errors, assumes that these are representative of future errors, and does not take into account the current level of uncertainty. Successive pairs of lighter-shaded areas around our central forecast represent 20 per cent probability bands.

Source: ONS, OBR

1.11 In the central forecast, limited second–round effects of externally driven inflation on nominal wages – and labour market looseness – means we expect no real earnings growth in 2027. Real wage growth then recovers to around ½ a per cent a year by 2030, as energy prices drop back. That medium-term growth rate is slightly below growth in output per worker as we expect firms to rebuild profit margins and the rate of return on capital to recover. Subdued real wage growth, alongside the combined effect of frozen income tax thresholds and higher nominal earnings growth, means we expect RHDI per person – an indicator of living standards – to fall slightly in the near term (Chart 1.5). RHDI per person then partially recovers as the energy price shock subsides. But, in 2030-31, it is only around 2 per cent above pre-pandemic levels and is about ¾ per cent below the level from our March forecast.

Chart 1.5: Real household disposable income per person

Source: ONS, OBR

1.12 Our central estimate of the level of potential output in the medium term is broadly unchanged from March. Recent GDP growth has been stronger than we expected – and we judge this to be structural – raising the starting level of potential output. We also have a slightly higher forecast for business investment – which raises capital deepening and productivity growth a little. Offsetting this, we expect net migration to be 70,000 lower on average per year, which has a small negative impact on our potential output forecast. But we judge that these migrants would have had broadly the same labour market characteristics as the domestic population, so the migration change has no impact on potential output on a per person basis. The higher starting point for potential output and the increased capital deepening forecast means that our central forecast for GDP per person has been revised up slightly in the medium term relative to March.

1.13 Our forecast for growth in total factor productivity in the medium term is unchanged relative to our March forecast. This is a highly uncertain judgement – and there is even uncertainty around the historical growth rates, which we explore further in Box 2.2. We have also assessed the impact of the Employment Rights Act 2025 in this forecast. We judge that while it will likely have a negative impact on potential output, the size of the effect is too uncertain to make an explicit adjustment at this forecast.

1.14 We expect real GDP growth of around 1¼ per cent this year – slightly higher than in March as the UK economy has, so far, been resilient to higher energy prices. We anticipate growth of around 1 per cent in 2027 – down about ½ a percentage point from March – as it takes time for higher global energy prices and increased interest rates to weigh on activity. Based on energy prices falling back, as suggested by the market pricing that underpins our forecast, we expect GDP growth to then recover and for the level of GDP to be broadly unchanged from our March forecast by 2030. Over the forecast, average annual real GDP growth is largely unchanged from March at around 1½ per cent. We judge the risks around our central forecast are broadly balanced. Looking at the distribution of historical forecast errors shown in Chart 1.6, in 2027, there is a one-in-five chance that real GDP growth could be higher than 2¼ per cent and a similar chance that it contracts.

Chart 1.6: Real GDP growth

Note: The chart shows annual real GDP growth. The forecast starts from 2026. The fan chart uses past forecast errors, assumes that these are representative of future errors, and does not take into account the current level of uncertainty. Successive pairs of lighter-shaded areas around our central forecast represent 20 per cent probability bands.

Source: ONS, OBR

1.15 In the central forecast, net additions to the housing stock fall from around 230,000 in 2025-26 to 220,000 in 2026-27, before increasing to 260,000 in 2030-31. Compared to March, we expect 190,000 fewer cumulative net additions over the forecast period. We have not revised our judgement that the 2024 planning reforms will have a positive impact on housebuilding. Instead, this change is driven by wider pressures in the construction sector because of weak demand, rising material costs and the impact of other regulatory changes.

1.16 We expect cumulative growth in the GDP deflator over the forecast to be around 1 percentage point higher than in March. This is due to higher energy prices received by domestic producers of oil and gas, and the second-round effects that higher inflation has through wage growth. This is the main driver of our upward revision to our central forecast for cumulative growth in nominal GDP over the forecast period – which is 1¼ percentage points higher than March. This drives higher growth in all of the main tax bases – labour income, corporate profits and nominal consumption.

Fiscal outlook

1.17 Relative to March, there is a moderate deterioration in the medium-term fiscal outlook, primarily due to the impact of higher debt interest costs. Public sector net borrowing is expected to be higher compared to March in every year of the forecast – by around £18 billion this year, £16 billion in 2027-28, and an average of £11 billion a year thereafter. As shown in Chart 1.7, the main drivers of the change in borrowing since March are:

  • Debt interest spending in the pre-measures forecast is expected to be £15 billion higher this year, driven by increased inflation raising the cost of index-linked gilts. Debt interest is then forecast to be £12 billion higher on average in the medium term, reflecting increased forecasts for gilt yields and Bank Rate.
  • Other areas of current public spending are £8 billion higher on average in the pre-measures forecast, including from higher inflation raising welfare spending and the cost of public service pensions. Capital spending items that are higher than forecast in March include departmental spending in 2026-27, and over the medium term local government and public corporations’ capital expenditure.
  • Upwards revisions to the pre-measures receipts forecast leave it £17 billion higher on average compared to March, largely driven by increased income tax and NICs, and corporation tax. This reflects higher inflation leading to upward revisions to the forecasts for nominal earnings and profits, respectively, alongside higher-than-expected receipts outturn so far this year.
  • The direct effects of policy measures increase borrowing by £8 billion in 2026-27, £4 billion in 2027-28, and £0.4 billion in 2028-29, and then reduce borrowing by £4 billion in 2029-30, £5 billion in 2030-31, and £12 billion in 2031-32. In the near term, this mainly reflects policies which increase spending. In the medium term, the reduction in borrowing is from a combination of tax increases and a reduction to the pre-measures spending assumption in 2031-32. The indirect effects of policy measures increase borrowing by a further £2 billion on average per year.
  • The changes to the forecast for the current deficit compared to March are very similar to the changes to borrowing, particularly in the medium term (Chart 1.7). The largest difference is in 2026-27 where a £12 billion forecast increase in capital spending, in large part due to departments spending more of their capital budgets than expected, does not affect the current budget. As a result, the current deficit is forecast to be only £7 billion higher than in March in 2026-27.

Chart 1.7: Changes in borrowing and the current budget deficit since March

Source: OBR

1.18 As a result of these changes, our central forecast is for borrowing to fall from 4.4 per cent of GDP (£134 billion) in 2025-26 to 4.2 per cent of GDP (£133 billion) this year. It is then expected to fall steadily over the medium term to reach 1.8 per cent of GDP (£67 billion) in 2030-31 and remain broadly unchanged thereafter (Chart 1.8). The outlook for borrowing is particularly uncertain as it represents the difference between around £1.5 trillion of both tax and spending by the end of the forecast period. Based on the distribution of historical shocks, there is a 60 per cent chance of PSNB being between a surplus of 1.0 per cent of GDP (£38 billion) and a deficit of 4.4 per cent of GDP (£168 billion) in 2031-32.

1.19 The right panel of Chart 1.8 shows that since 2022 plans to consolidate borrowing have been repeatedly delayed. This is due to a combination of subsequent unexpected shocks, lower-than-expected GDP growth, and discretionary policy choices to delay fiscal consolidation. Borrowing has now declined from around 5 per cent of GDP between 2022-23 and 2024-25, to 4.4 per cent of GDP in 2025-26. It is forecast to decline further to 4.2 per cent of GDP this year, and in the current central forecast is set to decline to just under 2 per cent of GDP in 2030-31. However, in the near-term the pace of this consolidation has again slowed, with borrowing between 2026-27 and 2028-29 now forecast to be around 0.5 per cent of GDP a year higher on average than forecast in November 2025. If the current forecast for borrowing to fall to around 2 per cent of GDP in 2029-30 is achieved, it would be 2 years later than forecast in November 2022.

Chart 1.8: Fan chart and successive forecasts for borrowing

Note: On the left panel, the solid black line shows our median forecast, with successive pairs of lighter shaded areas around it representing 20 per cent probability bands, with 20 per cent of the distribution outside the fan. On the right panel, forecast lines are presented as the nominal forecast for PSNB divided by the outturn and forecast for March-centred nominal GDP that are consistent with this forecast.

Source: ONS, OBR

1.20 In the central forecast, around sixty per cent of the projected 2.4 per cent of GDP decline in borrowing between 2026-27 and 2031-32 is driven by rising receipts as a share of GDP. A 0.9 per cent of GDP fall in spending accounts for the remainder:

  • Receipts are forecast to increase from 41.4 per cent of GDP this year to 42.9 per cent by 2031-32. The tax-to-GDP ratio is expected to surpass its historical high of 37.2 per cent in 2027-28 and to reach almost 6 per cent of GDP above the pre-pandemic level by 2031-32. The main drivers of this increase are rising personal taxes, capital taxes, and corporation tax.
  • Spending is forecast to increase from 45.6 per cent of GDP this year to a peak of 45.7 per cent in 2027-28, before declining to 44.7 per cent in 2031-32. The reduction in spending as a share of GDP is driven by planned reductions to departmental spending, which fall from 21.5 per cent of GDP in 2026-27, to 20.8 per cent in 2027-28 before falling further to 20.0 per cent in 2031-32.

Chart 1.9: Receipts and spending as a share of GDP

Source: OBR

1.21 In the pre-measures forecast, borrowing as a share of GDP starts to rise slightly from 1.9 per cent in 2030-31 to 2 per cent in 2031-32. This unusual feature is because some of the long-term pressures that we identified in the 2026 Fiscal risks and sustainability report (FRS) are already starting to bite. The increase reflects a combination of an increased pensioner population placing upward pressure on welfare spending, declining receipts from fuel duty due to the transition to electric vehicles, and increased spending on debt interest driven by significant redemptions of gilts at higher interest rates. This rise has been fully offset by policy measures announced by the Government at this Budget so that in the post-measures forecast borrowing as a share of GDP is flat between 2030-31 and 2031-32.

1.22 In the post-measures forecast, public sector net debt rises from 96 per cent of GDP in this year to peak at 97 per cent of GDP in 2028-29. It then falls back to 96 per cent in 2031-32, the final year of the forecast. This would be 0.6 per cent of GDP higher than in the final year of the March 2026 forecast. Net debt excluding the Bank of England is forecast to rise from 92 per cent of GDP this year to stabilise around 94 per cent in the final three years of the forecast. Debt on either measure is forecast to be no lower in 2031-32 than in the current year. Public sector net financial liabilities (PSNFL) are forecast to rise from 83 per cent of GDP this year to peak just under 84 per cent in 2027-28, and then to fall gradually in each subsequent year to 82 per cent at the end of the forecast.

Chart 1.10: Measures of the public sector balance sheet

Source: ONS, OBR

Performance against the Government’s fiscal targets

1.23 In the central forecast, the current budget deficit declines from 1.5 per cent of GDP (£47 billion) in 2025-26 to 1.3 per cent (£41 billion) this year (Chart 1.11). It is then forecast to improve gradually to reach a surplus of 0.4 per cent of GDP (£15 billion) in 2029-30, and then to stabilise at around 0.6 per cent on average in 2030-31 and 2031-32 (at £22 and £21 billion, respectively).

1.24 As a result, the Government’s fiscal mandate for the current budget to be in surplus in 2029-30 is met by a margin of £15 billion. This is £7 billion lower than when the fiscal rules were last formally assessed at Budget 2025. The probability of meeting the fiscal mandate is now estimated at 56 per cent, which compares to a probability of 59 per cent at Budget 2025. For the probability of meeting the fiscal mandate to be 60 or 70 per cent, it would require the current budget to be in surplus by around £25 and £50 billion respectively, in 2029-30.

1.25 The supplementary target, for public sector net financial liabilities to be falling as a share of GDP in 2029-30, is forecast to be met by a margin of £24 billion. This is unchanged relative to Budget 2025, when the fiscal targets were last assessed. For the probability of meeting the supplementary target to be 60 or 70 per cent, it would require PSNFL to be falling by 1.8 or 3.9 per cent of GDP in 2029-30 respectively, instead of the current forecast margin of a fall of 0.7 per cent of GDP.

Chart 1.11: Fan charts for the current budget deficit and PSNFL

The solid black line shows our median forecast, with successive pairs of lighter shaded areas around it representing 20 per cent probability bands, with 20 per cent of the distribution outside the fan.

Source: OBR

Risks and uncertainties

1.26 These margins against the fiscal targets are very small compared to the inherent risk and uncertainty around any medium-term fiscal forecast. The central forecast is an estimate of the middle of a range of possible outcomes, which means outturns substantially below and above our central forecast are likely and of broadly equal probability. Small percentage changes in the forecast for either tax or spending can lead to large percentage changes in borrowing. Since the pandemic, the average revision to pre-measures borrowing in the third year of the forecast has been £18 billion. The median three-year-ahead difference between forecast and outturn, since 2010, has been £34 billion. The margins against the fiscal mandate set out above are below the average margin of close to £30 billion held by Chancellors since 2010.

1.27 Key risks to our economy forecast include:

  • The evolution of the conflict in the Middle East, and its implications for energy prices, interest rates and inflation. Energy markets remain volatile – at the time of finalising this report, oil and gas futures pricing was close to the top of the range observed since the beginning of the Middle East conflict. Scenario analysis in Chapter 7 illustrates that if energy prices were to peak at a higher level and remain elevated for longer, we would expect increased inflation, weaker near-term GDP growth and a potential tightening of financial conditions. This could raise borrowing by as much as £17 billion in 2027-28, and by £6 billion in 2031-32, alongside an erosion of real-terms departmental spending power. If energy prices return to our March forecast path, we would expect more rapid disinflation, stronger near-term GDP growth and a potential easing of financial conditions – which could result in a decrease in borrowing of around £7 billion by 2031-32. The fiscal impacts would be particularly dependent on changes in financial conditions, the extent of pass-through of an inflation shock to wages given tax thresholds are currently frozen, and any policy response to inflationary pressures on departmental spending.
  • The outlook for productivity growth is a key risk to the economic and fiscal forecasts. The development and adoption of artificial intelligence could have a significant positive effect – our central forecast is for it to boost productivity growth by 0.2 percentage points a year by the medium term – but the scale and timing of any effect is highly uncertain. Revised ONS data on average hours, discussed in Box 2.2, suggest that UK productivity growth slowed after the financial crisis, but that the magnitude of the slowdown was closer to international comparators than previously suggested. In the November 2025 EFO, we illustrated that if annual productivity growth were 0.5 percentage points higher or lower than our central forecast in the medium term, borrowing could be around £50 billion lower or £40 billion higher by 2030-31.
  • There are several sources of uncertainty around the labour market outlook. There is significant uncertainty around the size and composition of future migration flows, given the post-Brexit change in the migration regime and the recent spike and subsequent fall in net migration. And there are risks around the outlook for unemployment. In the March EFO, we produced scenarios on the fiscal implications of alternative paths for the labour market. These suggested that if unemployment returned to its estimated equilibrium rate two years earlier than the central forecast, borrowing could be £16 billion a year lower on average, or if it instead peaked at around 7 per cent, borrowing could be an average of £20 billion a year higher than our central forecast.

1.28 Key additional risks to the fiscal forecast include:

  • The cost of servicing government debt – which had already increased sharply since 2021, before rising further in recent months following the start of the current Middle East conflict. Debt interest spending has risen from £39 billion (1.7 per cent of GDP) in 2019-20 to £109 billion (3.5 per cent of GDP) in 2025-26. It is now forecast to rise to £160 billion (4.2 per cent of GDP) in 2031-32. Gilt yields have recently been volatile, with the ten-year yield ranging from around 4.5 per cent to 5.5 per cent since March. But yields within this range are not unusual by historical standards. A 1 percentage point change in gilt yields and Bank Rate expectations across the forecast could lead to a medium-term change in borrowing of £16 billion.
  • There are a number of pressures on departmental spending. Since the Spending Review envelope was set, higher inflation has reduced the real value of the original spending limits by £7 billion each year. There are further pressures in the later years of the forecast for which departmental spending limits have not been set. Based on the Government’s assumption for total spending over this period – after taking into account policy commitments in areas including the NHS, defence and schools – spending would have to be £11 billion higher in 2031-32 to prevent ‘unprotected’ areas of day-to-day spending from falling in real terms from 2028-29.
    In the longer term, there are further pressures on public spending plans, including from the Government’s commitment to reform the social care system and to spend 3.5 per cent of GDP on defence by 2035.
  • There is also uncertainty around the outlook for welfare spending, especially related to health and disability benefits which have seen very significant rises in caseloads since the pandemic. In the central forecast, we assume that caseloads will continue to rise but at a slower rate. If they were to continue to rise at the post-pandemic growth rate, this could add around £25 billion to welfare spending by 2031-32. Or if caseload growth were to slow to pre-pandemic rates, then spending could be around £13 billion lower by 2031-32.
  • The tax-to-GDP ratio is expected to surpass its historical high of 37.2 per cent in 2027-28 and continue increasing in every year of the forecast. A higher level of the tax take increases the risk that incentives within the tax system constrain economic activity by more than expected. The single largest driver of the increase in the tax-GDP-ratio is from rising income tax and NICs which are highly sensitive to growth in nominal earnings, especially with personal tax thresholds frozen. Increased revenue from capital taxes is also a key component of the increase but the expected yield is highly sensitive to both equity prices and uncertain behavioural responses from affected taxpayers. The forecast also assumes the tax gap – a measure of tax compliance – will decline over the forecast period following a recent period where it has remained broadly flat.

1.29 The Government has taken some steps to address previously identified fiscal risks. The recently-announced reforms to the triple lock should significantly reduce the projected increase in the cost of state pension uprating over the long term relative to the previous policy. This is because the reforms remove the ‘ratchet’ effect whereby any increase in the value of pensions relative to earnings are permanently embedded and compound over time. However, alongside this reform, the Government also announced an intention to expand state provision of social care. The overall impact of these announcements on the public finances over the long term, therefore, depends on the additional cost of the social care reforms which is dependent on the final policy design and the timing of its introduction. In addition, the fiscal mandate now targets the third year of the forecast. This means that, from the next spending review, detailed spending allocations will be required for the year in which the fiscal targets bind which should reduce the risks around meeting these targets.

1.30 In practice, uncertainties around the central forecast are likely to be different to those in the past and go beyond the ones described above. As recent years show, there is a significant risk of unforeseeable adverse shocks having a large negative impact on the economy and public finances. Any such shocks over the forecast period, during which debt is forecast to remain elevated at approximately 95 per cent of GDP, would place further pressure on the UK’s fiscal sustainability.

1.31 Our 2026 FRS showed that fiscal pressures are likely to build over the long term. The drivers of this included an ageing population, wider health cost pressures, commitments to increase defence spending, and the fiscal costs of the transition to net zero. Long-term projections such as these are highly uncertain. However, in nearly all the scenarios we produced, including with different assumptions for long-term policy, debt would move on to an unsustainable upward trajectory within the next couple of decades unless policy action was taken to prevent this.

1.32 In the baseline long-term scenario in the FRS, debt moved to an unsustainable upward trajectory in the 2040s (Chart 1.12). This was based on the plans for fiscal consolidation in the March 2026 forecast, in which the primary balance – a measure that excludes debt interest spending – was projected to be in surplus by 1.5 per cent of GDP by 2030-31. If achieved, this would be the largest primary surplus since 2000-01. As set out above, since 2022, successive governments have set out similar plans for medium-term consolidation which have not subsequently been achieved. If we assume that the primary deficit in 2030-31 was at the same level as in 2025-26, then the unsustainable upward trajectory of debt would start in the 2030s. This underlines the importance for fiscal sustainability of delivering the current planned medium-term consolidation.

Chart 1.12: Long term debt scenarios: 2026 Fiscal risks and sustainability report

Source: OBR

Chapter 2: Economic outlook

Summary of the economy forecast

2.1 The UK’s economic position remains challenging. A series of major adverse shocks since the global financial crisis, interacting with underlying structural trends, have contributed to persistently weak growth in per person GDP, episodes of sustained high inflation and subdued growth in real household disposable incomes. A broad range of factors, many of which originate outside the UK, will influence the UK’s future economic performance. Further adverse shocks may prolong or worsen recent weak growth, while stronger productivity growth, for example from advances in artificial intelligence (AI), may offset or reverse recent trends.

2.2 We finalised our March 2026 forecast just before the start of the current conflict in the Middle East. That forecast was conditioned on market pricing for Bank Rate to be cut twice by the end of this year. We expected inflation to fall back to around 2 per cent by the end of this year and GDP growth to rise to around 1½ per cent in 2027. Since then, energy prices have risen substantially – though by less than following the Russian invasion of Ukraine. As the UK is a net importer of energy, this constitutes a negative terms-of-trade shock and has already raised CPI inflation. While real GDP growth over the year to date has been more resilient than expected, the labour market remains weak, with job vacancies continuing to fall and some data suggesting an ongoing drop in the number of payrolled employees. Market pricing suggests interest rates will be significantly higher than anticipated in March.

2.3 Table 2.1 summarises the key conditioning assumptions and judgements which underpin our latest central forecast. Our central forecast for real GDP growth in 2027 is 1 per cent – ½ a percentage point below our March forecast, as a result of higher energy prices and interest rates. We expect CPI inflation to peak at close to 4 per cent in early 2027, driven largely by the direct and indirect effects of higher energy prices, with relatively modest second-round effects through earnings. We expect no growth in real earnings in 2027 and 2028, and a slight fall in real household disposable income (RHDI) per person in the near term. Market pricing suggests energy prices will fall sharply from mid-2027. This would contribute to inflation falling back to its 2 per cent target in our central forecast by 2028, and to the level of real GDP in 2030 being broadly unchanged from our March forecast. But we expect RHDI per person to continue its recent trend of weak growth such that in 2029-30, it is only expected to be 1 per cent higher than its pre-pandemic level.

2.4 Our central economy forecast is a point estimate within a wide range of possible outcomes. It is also a conditional forecast based on market pricing in mid-August for current and future energy prices and interest rates. Energy prices have been highly volatile and, at the point at which we finalised this Economic and fiscal outlook (EFO) publication, had increased significantly compared to those levels. Therefore, we set out alternative energy price scenarios in Chapter 7 to assess the impact that higher or lower energy prices could have for the economic and fiscal outlook. We also discuss a wide range of other risks around our central forecast throughout this EFO, including through the use of fan charts of probability ranges and sensitivity analysis. One of our most important and most uncertain judgements is around the outlook for productivity growth. Box 2.2 explores the recent ONS revisions to historical productivity, which show that even the recent evolution of productivity growth is highly uncertain.
