Conditioning assumptions

2.5 We conditioned our economy forecast on market pricing over the 10 working days to 19 August. The final pre-measures fiscal forecast is produced after the pre-measures economy forecast, enabling us to use a later reading for interest rates in the fiscal forecast – based on the five working days to 15 September. Below we describe the conditioning assumptions used in the economy forecast, consistent with the earlier window, and briefly explain where the fiscal assumptions differ.

Energy prices

2.6 As Chart 2.1 illustrates, wholesale energy prices have increased significantly since our March forecast, largely as a result of the conflict in the Middle East. Energy prices have also been volatile over this period, with Brent crude spot prices ranging between 72 and 114 dollars a barrel (with the highs close to the 2022 peak) and UK natural gas spot prices ranging between 97 and 216 pence a therm (still significantly below the 2022 peak).

2.7 Our central forecast, shown in Chart 2.1, is based on market pricing over the 10 working days to 19 August. Based on those prices, over the forecast period:

  • Oil prices fall from a peak of 95 dollars a barrel in the second quarter of this year to between 70 and 75 dollars a barrel by 2028, and remain around that level thereafter. Compared to our March forecast, oil prices are 30 per cent higher in 2026 and 2027 and around 8 per cent higher in 2030.
  • Natural gas prices reach around 150 pence a therm at the end of this year, more than double our March forecast but significantly below the 2022 peak. They then fall to around 95 pence a therm by late 2027 and average just under 70 pence a therm from mid-2028 (12 per cent higher than the March forecast).
  • Electricity prices peak at around 125 pounds a megawatt hour at the end of this year (around 65 per cent higher than in the March forecast) before falling to under 70 pounds per megawatt hour from mid-2028 (5 per cent below our March forecast). The reduction in medium-term electricity prices since March, despite the rise in medium-term gas prices, illustrates their continued gradual decoupling, discussed in Box 2.1.

2.8 Oil and gas prices have increased further since we took market expectations on the 10 working days to 19 August, and remain volatile. Since the start of the conflict in the Middle East, futures prices for 2027 have ranged between 70 and 95 dollars a barrel for oil and 75 and 180 pence a therm for gas. At the time of finalising this report, oil and gas futures were close to the top end of this range. We set out scenarios which assess the impact of higher and lower oil and gas prices for the economic and fiscal outlook in Chapter 7.

Chart 2.1: Energy prices

Note: October 2026 forecast uses the 10 working days to 19 August and March 2026 forecast uses the 10 working days to 22 January. Range since Middle East conflict is range between 2 March and 20 October.

Source: Bloomberg, Ofgem, OBR

Box 2.1: The impact of energy price shocks on economic activity

Oil and gas prices have risen significantly since the start of the current conflict in the Middle East. This comes four years after the spike in oil and particularly gas prices following the Russian invasion of Ukraine in 2022. The impact of an energy price shock on UK economic activity depends on a range of factors beyond the change in wholesale prices – including longer-term trends in the UK’s energy mix and energy intensity, the UK’s dependence on energy imports, the pass-through of wholesale prices to those paid by firms and households, and underlying economic conditions.

Chart A shows the annual nominal change in fossil fuel prices weighted by the UK’s changing relative total consumption of coal, oil, and natural gas over time. Fossil fuel prices rose by 83 per cent in the year to September 2026, just under a third of the peak rate seen in 2022, and less than half of that seen in the 1973 shock.a There is some evidence that ample pre-shock supply and inventories, supply responses elsewhere, and reduced global demand – especially from China – have helped cushion the initial price shock in 2026.b

Chart A: Fossil fuel price inflation

Note: Shaded areas represent historical energy price shocks based on Dhingra, S., Running on empty: Climate risks and the fragility of global energy and food supply chains, Bank of England, June 2026. The last data point on the chart is for September 2026.

Source: Bloomberg, DESNZ, World Bank, OBR

Structural changes in the UK’s energy usage influence the impact of energy price shocks on economic activity.c Since the 1970s:

The UK’s energy mix has become more diverse, with the share of energy consumption from fossil fuels falling from 96 per cent in 1970 to 75 per cent in 2025 (Chart B, left panel). Coal has been nearly eliminated as an energy source and replaced by a combination of renewables and gas, with the latter’s supply disrupted by both the 2022 and 2026 shocks. In the long term, greater electrification of transport and heating is expected to shift direct energy demand further from oil and gas, and towards the increasingly renewable inputs used to produce electricity.d

The UK’s energy intensity has fallen significantly, with energy consumption per unit of real GDP now one quarter of its value in 1970 (Chart B, right panel). All else equal, this reduces the economy-wide impact of a given energy price shock. Reduced energy intensity reflects improvements in energy efficiency and changes in the composition of economic activity.

The UK’s reliance on energy imports has varied significantly. The right panel of Chart B illustrates how North Sea production turned the UK from a substantial net importer in the 1970s into a net exporter, before declining production reversed this from the mid-2000s. This drives the size and sign of the terms-of-trade shock when international prices vary, and therefore the scale of the effect on real incomes.

Chart B: Sources and dependence of UK energy since 1970

Note: In the right panel, energy intensity is calculated as tonnes in oil equivalent divided by real GDP (£ million, 2023 prices). Net energy imports are measured as a percentage of inland energy consumption of primary fuels plus marine bunkers. The latest data in both charts is for 2025.
Source: DESNZ, OBR

The pass-through of fossil fuel prices to electricity prices depends in part on marginal pricing, because the wholesale electricity price is set by the last and most expensive generator needed to meet demand. In the UK, this is often relatively expensive gas-fired generation, so higher gas prices can feed through to wholesale electricity prices even when most generation does not depend on gas. This link has weakened recently as the share of renewable generation has increased.e Furthermore, wholesale electricity and gas costs were only expected to make up 47 per cent of the average domestic consumer’s bill in the fourth quarter of 2026,f with the remainder from a combination of taxes, levies, and other costs. Consumers have been shielded from higher prices in recent years by bespoke government support and fuel duty not being uprated. Government support in 2022-23 totalled £52.2 billion.g

Underlying economic conditions also play a significant role in the ultimate economic impact of an energy price shock. Chart C illustrates that the labour market was considerably looser at the start of 2026 than at the start of 2022, with the vacancies-to-unemployment ratio at less than half of its 2022 level. Our estimated negative output gap of -1.0 per cent of potential output at the start of 2026, compared to a positive output gap of 2.1 per cent at the start of 2022, implies a much greater degree of spare capacity in the economy. Monetary policy was also looser in 2022, when Bank Rate was initially 0.5 per cent compared to 3.75 per cent. The 2022 shock also came when inflation was already high and rising, amid severe global supply chain disruption as the global economy rebalanced after the pandemic. Relative to 2022, these factors should reduce the risk of the current shock propagating strongly through wages and creating significant domestically generated inflation. However, the scale of second-round effects remains uncertain: for example, inflation expectations may now be more responsive to energy and food price movements after several years of above-target inflation.h

Chart C: Initial conditions for previous energy price shocks

Note: Shaded areas represent the historical energy price shocks from Dhingra, S., Running on empty: Climate risks and the fragility of global energy and food supply chains, Bank of England, June 2026. The vacancies-to-unemployment ratio is a three-month rolling average.

Source: ONS, OBR

As the UK is a net importer of energy, the recent rise in global energy prices represents a negative terms-of-trade shock that will lower real incomes. But this latest shock is smaller than the 2022 episode and there is more spare capacity in the economy, which should reduce the peak and persistence of inflation. The size and limited duration of the shock, declining fossil-fuel exposure, and the decoupling of electricity and gas prices together mean we do not expect a permanent hit to potential output from the energy price paths conditioning our forecast.

a) The year-on-year change in fossil fuel prices in 2022 partly reflects an increase from a lower base after the pandemic.

b) For example, see D’Orazio, A., S. Emiliozzi, F. Ferriani, A. Gazzani, M. Taboga and G. Veronese, Energy markets under chokepoint stress: The 2026 Hormuz shock, VoxEU, CEPR, September 2026.

c) We previously explored these trends in depth in our 2022 and 2023 Fiscal risks and sustainability reports.

d) The market pricing for electricity futures we use to condition our forecast in the near term is likely to account for this decoupling.

e) The proportion of time in which gas sets the wholesale price for electricity is not directly observed and therefore uncertain, but recent government estimates suggest this has fallen from around 90 per cent of the time in the early 2020s, to around 60 per cent today. See McCluskey, K., R. Carmichael, A. Rhodes, R. Gross and W. Blyth, The cost of electricity in the UK: drivers, challenges and opportunities for reform, UK Energy Research Centre, July 2026.

f) See ‘Summary of changes to the energy price cap: 1 October to 31 December 2026’ on Ofgem, Energy price cap (default tariff) levels (accessed October 2026). The wholesale cost share of a consumer’s bill is for direct debit customers and includes Contracts for Difference (CfDs).

g) See Box 3.1 of our October 2023 Forecast evaluation report.

h) For example, see Mann, C., Holding the anchor in turbulent waters, Bank of England, March 2025.

Interest rates

2.9 Chart 2.2 shows that market pricing for Bank Rate and gilt yields has increased significantly since our March forecast:[1]

  • Our central economy forecast is conditioned on Bank Rate reaching just over 4¼ per cent by mid-2027 and just over 4½ per cent by the end of the forecast. On average, this is just under ¾ of a percentage point higher than our March forecast. In our fiscal forecast, Bank Rate is on average just over a further ¼ of a percentage point higher.
  • Gilt yields have also risen substantially across all maturities since our March forecast. On average, yields are just over ½ a percentage point higher in our economy forecast, and a further ¼ of a percentage point higher in our fiscal forecast.

2.10 Since the start of the conflict in the Middle East, interest rates have fluctuated significantly. Between the onset of the conflict and 20 October, market pricing for Bank Rate in late-2027 has ranged from 3½ per cent to 5 per cent. Gilt yields at the 10-year mark have ranged from around 4½ per cent to 5½ per cent over the same period. The assumptions in our fiscal forecast and the latest market pricing at the time we finalised this report sit close to the top of these ranges.

Chart 2.2: Interest rates

Note: October 2026 economy forecast uses 10 working days to 19 August, October 2026 fiscal forecast uses five working days to 15 September, and March 2026 forecast uses the 10 working days to 22 January. Range since Middle East conflict is range between 2 March and 20 October.

Source: Bank of England, OBR

2.11 Chart 2.3 shows that nominal and real 10-year government bond yields have risen in all G7 economies since March. This is likely to partly reflect the impact of higher energy prices on inflation and on monetary policy expectations, and rises in real yields. Some commentators have also pointed to sustained, large fiscal deficits in many advanced economies and competition for financing from increased AI investment as factors pushing up yields. The increases in UK nominal and real gilt yields since March (around 1¼ and ¾ of a percentage point respectively) are within the range of other G7 economies, suggesting recent UK market movements largely reflect common developments across advanced economies.

Chart 2.3: International comparison of 10-year government bond yields

Note: On both charts, the first data point is 27 February 2026, and the last data point is 20 October 2026.

Source: Bloomberg, OBR

2.12 However, interest rates at all maturities in the UK have been consistently among the highest in the G7 since 2022, having been toward the middle of the pack in the decade prior. Drivers of this likely include:

  • Recent episodes of higher and more persistent inflation than in other advanced economies. CPI inflation in the UK averaged 9.1 per cent in 2022, the highest in the G7 versus an average of 6.4 per cent across the rest of the group. UK inflation has remained among the highest in the G7 since then.
  • The UK’s higher level of borrowing resulting in a larger increase in net debt than other advanced economies. Between 2019 and 2025, the UK’s net debt-to-GDP ratio on an internationally comparable basis increased by 19 percentage points, significantly above the 5 percentage point average for other G7 economies.
  • Structural changes in the composition of demand for gilts, as set out in our 2025 Fiscal risks and sustainability report (FRS). The decline of defined benefit pension schemes and the Bank of England’s continued quantitative tightening have reduced gilt holdings of pension funds and of the central bank. We found that expected shifts in gilt demand toward more price-sensitive foreign investors could increase the overall interest rate on UK government debt by close to 1 percentage point over the next 50 years, though markets may already have priced in some of this impact.

2.13 Despite these substantial rises over recent years, interest rates are not high by historical standards. The 2010s, in which Bank Rate was close to the zero-lower bound and quantitative easing reduced longer term gilt rates to multi-decade lows, was historically unprecedented. Current levels are close to historical experience – since 1935, 10-year gilt yields have averaged around 6 per cent.

Equity prices

2.14 Equity prices, measured by the FTSE All-Share index, are around 1½ per cent higher on average than in our March forecast. We assume that equity prices grow in line with nominal GDP over the forecast. Valuations, measured by price-to-earnings (P/E) ratios, remain around historical averages in the UK, while US equity valuations remain elevated. However, as global equity markets are highly correlated, a potential correction in equity valuations elsewhere poses a downside risk to our forecast. We explored the potential impacts of a negative shock to equity prices in our Autumn 2025 EFO.

World economy, trade and the exchange rate

2.15 Our central forecast, based on the IMF’s July 2026 World Economic Outlook Update and April 2026 World Economic Outlook (WEO), is for global GDP to increase by 3.0 per cent this year, down from 3.4 per cent in 2025. This is unchanged from our March forecast. The IMF July WEO Update noted that the impact of tariffs has been more muted than expected, while the energy shock has been partly offset by momentum in the technology sector due to the growth and adoption of AI. Global GDP growth thereafter averages around 3 per cent a year. Global trade is expected to increase by 3½ per cent in 2026 and 4¼ per cent in 2027 before averaging around 3 per cent a year over the rest of the forecast.

2.16 US tariff policy continues to be restrictive compared to recent experience. Changes in US tariff policy since July have reduced the relative advantage previously held by UK exporters. Chapter 3 discusses the potential impacts of recent changes to UK trade policy.

2.17 Since our March forecast, the pound has appreciated ½ per cent against the dollar (to 1.35 dollars per pound) and 2 per cent against the euro (to 1.17 euros per pound). We assume that exchange rates are unchanged over our forecast. As a result, the trade-weighted sterling effective exchange rate remains about 1¼ per cent higher across the forecast period compared to our March forecast.

Fiscal policy

2.18 In the central forecast, public sector net borrowing falls from 4.4 per cent of GDP in 2025-26 to 1.8 per cent of GDP in 2031-32, but is higher than in our March forecast in every year. The primary balance (public sector non-interest revenue minus non-interest expenditure) moves from a deficit of 1.5 per cent of GDP in 2025-26 to a surplus by 2028-29, and reaches a surplus of 1.7 per cent of GDP in 2031-32. The drivers of reduced borrowing over the forecast are discussed in Chapter 6.

2.19 Policy measures increase borrowing modestly as a share of GDP from 2026-27 to 2028-29, and slightly reduce it thereafter. We judge that these changes in borrowing will not have a material impact on real GDP growth that would justify an explicit adjustment to the forecast. This is due to the relatively moderate size and profile of the changes in borrowing, and because some measures were announced prior to the Budget (see paragraph 3.9). The wider impacts of policy measures are discussed in more detail in Chapter 3.

Inflation

2.20 CPI inflation has fallen by less than we expected in March, reaching 3.1 per cent in the third quarter of 2026, 1 percentage point above our March forecast. In the 12 months to September 2026, CPI inflation was 3.5 per cent. Higher-than-expected inflation has largely been driven by the direct effects of higher utilities and fuel prices.

2.21 As illustrated in Chart 2.4, we expect that the recent rise in energy prices will keep CPI inflation above the Bank of England’s 2 per cent target in 2026 and 2027. In our central forecast, CPI inflation increases from around 3.1 per cent in 2026 to 3.4 per cent in 2027, with a quarterly peak of 3.8 per cent in early 2027.[2] Policy measures reduce CPI inflation by 0.1 percentage points in 2027 and increase it by around the same amount in 2028 – this is discussed in more detail in Chapter 3. We expect inflation to fall back to target halfway through 2028 in our central forecast, as energy prices fall based on the market pricing that our economy forecast is conditioned on.

2.22 The near-term outlook for inflation is significantly higher than in our March forecast, primarily driven by:

  • the direct effect of higher energy prices on fuel prices and utilities – in our forecast the Ofgem energy price cap peaks at around £1,875 in Q1 2027;[3]
  • the indirect effects of higher energy prices on the production and transportation costs of other goods and services, compounded by broader commodity price pressures – including from elevated fertiliser prices and increases in cereal and metals prices – which could feed through to the price of food and other tradable goods; and
  • some second-round effects from higher inflation through earnings, as workers attempt to protect their real wages – but we assume that these will be smaller than for the 2022 energy shock because the labour market is significantly looser, the rise in energy prices is smaller and market pricing suggests it will be less persistent.

2.23 There is significant uncertainty around our inflation forecast, mainly due to the evolving situation in the Middle East. Our central forecast is conditioned on market pricing for oil and gas from mid-August. We explore scenarios for different energy price paths in Chapter 7. If energy prices are significantly higher than the path assumed in our forecast, we would expect larger direct and indirect effects on CPI inflation, and potentially larger second-round effects. Conversely, an orderly resolution of the conflict could mean that inflation falls more quickly than expected. The extent to which higher energy prices generate second-round effects through wages is also highly uncertain and particularly important for the fiscal forecast, given the high effective tax rate on labour income and that income tax thresholds are currently frozen.

2.24 Other risks to the CPI inflation forecast include the degree to which a particularly strong El Niño phenomenon disrupts global food production and puts upward pressure on food prices. Based purely on past forecast errors, Chart 2.4 suggests there is a one-in-five chance that CPI inflation in 2027 will be above 4½ per cent and a similar probability it will be below 2½ per cent.

Chart 2.4: CPI inflation

Note: The chart shows annual average CPI inflation. The forecast starts from 2026. The fan chart uses past forecast errors, assumes that these are representative of future errors, and does not take into account the current level of uncertainty. Successive pairs of lighter-shaded areas around our central forecast represent 20 per cent probability bands.

Source: ONS, OBR

2.25 In our central forecast, RPI inflation increases from around 3½ per cent this year to around 4¼ per cent in 2027, before falling back to 3 per cent in 2028, broadly in line with the shape of the CPI inflation path. We then expect RPI inflation to drop to 2¼ per cent in 2030 as the ONS converges the RPI measure to CPIH (see Box 2.3 of our October 2024 EFO for a more detailed explanation of this change).

2.26 We expect the GDP deflator, which measures the price of all domestically produced goods and services, to grow by 3 per cent in 2026. The GDP deflator is affected by energy prices directly to the extent that energy is produced in the UK and indirectly to the extent that energy prices create second-round effects on the price of other goods and services through increased average earnings. We expect the growth in the deflator to fall to around 2¼ per cent in 2027 and around 2 per cent on average from 2028 onward, as energy prices – and, in particular, the second-round effects of those price rises – fall back. Compared to our March forecast, we project that cumulative growth in the GDP deflator between 2025-26 and 2030-31, an important determinant of our receipts forecast, will be around 1 percentage point higher.

Earnings and income growth

Average earnings

2.27 Nominal and real average earnings growth in 2025 has been revised down by around a percentage point by the ONS since our March forecast. In our central forecast, we anticipate that nominal earnings growth will continue to slow in the near term, partly due to looser labour market conditions (left panel of Chart 2.5). But we expect a more gradual slowdown than in March, reflecting a judgement that there will be some second-round effects of higher inflation through wage growth, as workers attempt to protect their real wages. We judge that these effects will be modest relative to the previous energy price spike in 2022, as a significantly looser labour market restricts workers’ capacity to bargain for higher wages. This is reflected in recent survey evidence, with firms increasingly expecting limited upward pressure on wages.[4] The market pricing underpinning our forecast also suggests that the increase in energy prices will be smaller and less persistent than in 2022, which should further limit the size of second-round effects.

2.28 Second-round effects on wage growth have particularly important fiscal implications, given that income tax thresholds are currently frozen and labour income has the highest effective tax rate of any tax base. The size of these effects is uncertain and will depend on a number of factors including: the size of the energy price shock relative to the market pricing that our forecast is conditioned on; whether workers’ inflation expectations respond more or less than we assume to rising inflation; the monetary and fiscal policy response to the shock; and the degree to which a relatively loose labour market restricts workers’ capacity to bargain for higher wages. We explore the impact of stronger second-round effects in our energy price scenarios in Chapter 7.

2.29 Taken together with our forecast for inflation, this means that we expect lower near-term growth in real earnings than in March. We expect no growth in real earnings in 2027 and 2028. Over the medium term, real wage growth recovers to around ½ a per cent a year by 2030 in our central forecast (right panel of Chart 2.5). This is slightly weaker than growth in output per worker as we expect firms to rebuild profit margins, which allows the rate of return on capital to recover. This, in part, reflects our assessment in our October 2024 forecast – we expect firms to pass on an increasing share of the Autumn Budget 2024 employer NICs increase to workers, rather than absorbing it in lower profits.

Chart 2.5: Nominal and real earnings growth

Note: Nominal earnings are wages and salaries divided by the number of employees. Real earnings are deflated using the private consumption deflator.

Source: ONS, OBR

Real household disposable income

2.30 In our central forecast, real household disposable income (RHDI) per person, a useful though incomplete measure of living standards,[5] falls slightly in the near term. The left panel of Chart 2.6 shows the contributions to growth in RHDI per person, with labour income, non-labour income, and social benefits all boosting income. But across last year and this year, these factors are outweighed by higher prices and taxes on income and wealth. In terms of economic drivers, this near-term weakness is driven by the negative terms-of-trade shock, which pushes down on real wage growth, but up on nominal wage growth. The latter then weighs on RHDI because frozen income tax thresholds increase the degree to which effective tax rates rise as nominal earnings increase.

2.31 In the medium term, the expected fall-back in energy prices means RHDI per person partly recovers. Growth over the last four years of the forecast averages around ¾ of a per cent a year, broadly in line with the average since 2010. But the right panel of Chart 2.6 shows that the level of RHDI per person is forecast to remain more than ¾ of a per cent below the March profile in 2030-31.

Chart 2.6: Real household disposable income per person

Source: ONS, OBR

2.32 Our central forecast represents a continuation of the historically weak growth in RHDI per person since the global financial crisis. Chart 2.7 shows the rolling 10-year average annual growth in RHDI per person. This slowed significantly in the aftermath of the financial crisis, due to lower growth in productivity and real wages. Growth reached a historic low over the 10 years to 2025-26, averaging just ¼ per cent a year, and we expect it to fall further in the near term. By 2029-30, our central forecast sees growth over the prior 10 years averaging less than 0.1 per cent a year. As a result, 10 years on from the onset of the pandemic, RHDI per person would be just 1 per cent higher than its pre-pandemic level.

Chart 2.7: Rolling 10-year average growth in RHDI per person

Note: Compound average growth rate over a 10-year period. 2025-26 is the final year of outturn data, with forecast ending in 2031-32.
Source: ONS, OBR

Potential output, productivity and labour supply

2.33 In our central forecast, we expect potential output growth to fall from around 1½ per cent this year to about 1 per cent next year, as higher energy prices weigh on productive potential. We also expect lower net migration to slow population and labour supply growth, but to have little impact on GDP or real incomes per person. Chart 2.8 shows that we expect potential output growth to then rise to around 1½ per cent in the medium term as market pricing implies a fall back in energy prices.

Chart 2.8: Potential output growth

Source: OBR

2.34 Relative to March, our central forecast is for the level of potential output to be broadly unchanged in the medium term. A downward revision to net migration is offset by a higher starting level of potential output driven by strong recent GDP outturns, which we judge to be structural; and a higher forecast for business investment which slightly raises capital deepening. In per adult terms, potential output is expected to be slightly higher in the medium term than in March as the net migration revision has no impact.

Labour supply

2.35 Labour supply growth is expected to slow from around ¾ of a per cent in 2026 to around 1/3 of a per cent a year over the medium term, marginally lower than in March. The slowdown over the forecast reflects net migration falling this year and then remaining more in line with the previous decade’s average over the next few years, alongside the impact of the ageing population on average hours and participation.

Adult population

2.36 Our forecast for the adult population is based on historical data from the Labour Force Survey (LFS), births and deaths from the latest ONS National Population Projections (NPPs), and our own forecast for net migration. [6] Based on these, the size of the adult population is forecast to rise by around 320,000 people a year (around 150,000 via net migration), from 56.8 million in 2026 to 58.4 million in 2031. The adult population in 2030 is now expected to be around 50,000 lower than in our March forecast, as shown in the left panel of Chart 2.9, reflecting the downward revision to our net migration forecast partly offset by higher-than-expected outturn population data.

Net migration

2.37 Data published since we finalised the March forecast suggest that net migration will be lower than we previously assumed. The ONS estimates that net migration fell from 331,000 in 2024 to 171,000 in 2025, while visa data up to June 2026 continue to show declines across most visa types. Reflecting these latest data, and informed by our migration model, we expect net migration to fall to 120,000 in the year to mid-2026 (right panel of Chart 2.9).[7] The near-term decline reflects visa restrictions introduced since 2023 alongside higher emigration following past high levels of immigration, especially among students. Net migration is then forecast to rise gradually to 230,000 by the year to mid-2031, in line with the ‘principal variant’ of the latest ONS population projections. This rise largely reflects our assumption that the net outflow of British nationals and EU nationals who arrived under the pre-Brexit immigration regime will gradually lessen. We judge our net migration forecast to be a reasonable central assumption based on recent outturn data, our modelling infrastructure, and current government policy.

Chart 2.9: Adult population and net migration

Source: ONS, OBR

2.38 Our net migration forecast is around 70,000 a year lower than in March. This has a small negative impact on the level of GDP and potential output in the medium term – but has no impact on per person incomes. The composition of migration is also important for economic and fiscal outcomes, with the following being important factors:

  • Demographics: on arrival, migrants are generally more likely to be of working age with around 80 per cent of migrants being aged 16-64 – compared to around 60 per cent for the wider UK population – in the principal variant of the ONS 2024-based NPPs.
  • Participation: on arrival, migrants tend to have a lower rate of participation at a given age than the domestic population. This generally offsets the effect of demographics and means that the average migrant tends to have a similar overall participation rate. See Box 2.3 of the March 2024 EFO and Box 2.3 of the November 2023 EFO for more detail on these assumptions.
  • Productivity: we assume migrants have broadly the same average rate of productivity as the domestic population. This judgement is highly uncertain as it depends on a range of factors related to the characteristics of migrants which we explored in more detail in Box 2.1 of the March 2026 EFO.

2.39 We consider all of these factors when assessing the impact on the labour market of revisions to our overall net migration forecast, as the composition of the migrant population will vary significantly across different entry routes.[8] The downward revision to net migration in this forecast is broad-based across various visa routes so we judge that this revision has no impact on the overall participation rate or level of productivity. This means that we assume that lower net migration will leave GDP per person, an indicator of average living standards, unchanged by the end of the forecast.

2.40 There remains considerable uncertainty around our net migration forecast, including the impact of previous policy changes on the outlook for student visas, the share of humanitarian arrivals from Ukraine that remain in the UK, and the future level of net migration of EU nationals. Potential changes to indefinite leave to remain policy, which are not currently reflected in our forecast as the policy details are not sufficiently certain, and changes to visa salary requirements, the details of which were confirmed after the deadline for inclusion in the economy forecast, may also affect net migration in future. We explored the economic impact of two alternative scenarios of higher and lower medium-term net migration in Box 2.3 of the March 2024 EFO. In these scenarios, we also demonstrated the impacts of using alternative assumptions for migrants’ productivity and participation in the labour force, which are significant.

Participation rate and average hours

2.41 Our forecasts for trend labour force participation and average hours are unchanged from March. The trend labour force participation rate is forecast to fall slightly from 63½ per cent in 2026 to 63¼ per cent in 2030, while trend average hours fall marginally from 32 hours a week in 2026 to 31¾ hours a week in 2030. Both decline because of our assumption that the population will continue to age, and that older people, on average, have lower participation rates and work fewer hours. The decline in average hours is more gradual than had been the case before the financial crisis, as we assume that the weakness in productivity growth since then limits workers’ capacity to use part of their increase in real wages to increase their amount of leisure time. The recent ONS revisions to historical average hours, which were published after we finalised our forecast, suggest that average hours growth has been lower, and output per hour higher, than we had previously thought. We discuss these revisions in detail in Box 2.2.

Productivity

2.42 We assume that trend productivity growth slows in 2027 to 0.7 per cent, largely because of the spike in energy prices.[9] Productivity growth is then forecast to rise to 1.1 per cent in 2031 as energy prices fall back. We assume the slightly higher medium term fossil fuel prices in financial market pricing do not reduce medium-term productivity, as firms adjust production processes and energy sources (see Box 2.1 for more details).

2.43 The main change to our forecast for trend productivity growth since March is a stronger contribution from capital deepening, largely reflecting a higher path for business investment following consistently stronger-than-expected outturns. This change, combined with slightly higher-than-expected growth in the first half of 2026, leaves the level of trend productivity around 0.2 per cent higher than in the March forecast in 2030. We have not changed our previous judgement that underlying trend total factor productivity growth reaches 0.8 per cent in 2030, which we explained in Briefing paper No.9: Forecasting productivity. The recent ONS revisions to productivity were published after we finalised this forecast, but we discuss them in Box 2.2. We will incorporate the impact of any changes, alongside other developments, in our next forecast.

Alternative measure of productivity

2.44 Labour market data remain a key source of uncertainty for the productivity outlook. Chart 2.10 compares an LFS-based measure of labour productivity with an alternative measure created by the Resolution Foundation, based on HMRC PAYE real time information on employees and administrative data on self-employment. The two measures now suggest similarly weak cumulative growth since the pandemic (shown in the right panel), despite considerably different paths to get there. This demonstrates the uncertainty around even recent trends in productivity growth. We remain cautious about interpreting trends from recent data from relatively short periods and we will continue to regularly review our productivity assumption to consider any new information, such as the component-based data discussed in Box 2.2.

Chart 2.10: Productivity (output per hour)

Note: Both measures use real GDP from Quarterly National Accounts published on 30 September. Alternative measure uses administrative employment data constructed by the Resolution Foundation (accessed on 25 September) and average hours data published on 18 August. LFS measures use total hours published on 18 August.

Source: ONS, Resolution Foundation, OBR

2.45 The development and adoption of AI is widely expected to boost future productivity growth, and we previously analysed this in Annex B of Briefing paper No.9: Forecasting productivity. Sectoral data can help to shed light on the degree to which AI is already enhancing UK productivity growth. Initially, it might be expected that ‘AI-producing’ sectors would experience an acceleration in productivity growth, and then over time this would spread to ‘AI-adopting’ sectors. According to the latest ONS Business Insights and Conditions Survey, only 30 per cent of businesses reported using at least one type of AI technology in September 2026, though this is up from 15 per cent two years ago.

2.46 While sectoral data over short periods can be highly volatile, data on output per worker based on administrative sources suggests recent growth in productivity has been broad-based.[10] Table 2.2 shows that growth between the final quarter of 2024 and the second quarter of 2026 has accelerated compared to previous periods in some of the sectors most associated with the production and use of AI services, such as IT and communications, and professional, scientific and technical services. But productivity growth was relatively strong in these sectors prior to the pandemic, and growth has also accelerated in sectors likely to be currently less affected by AI, such as accommodation and food, and wholesale and retail. We will continue to closely monitor the emerging evidence and how to reflect it in our forecasts for productivity and employment.

Box 2.2: Recent ONS revisions to historical economic data

In the period since we closed our forecast to new data,a the ONS has released revised historical National Accounts and hourly productivity data.

Blue Book-consistent Quarterly National Accounts figures

The Blue Book is the ONS’s annual update to the sources and methods used in the UK National Accounts, and the Quarterly National Accounts published on 30 September were consistent with the 2026 Blue Book. Real GDP growth between 1997 and the second quarter of 2026 has been revised upward by an annual average of 0.1 percentage points. Growth in 2025 was revised down by 0.1 percentage points, and growth in the first half of 2026 revised up by the same amount. Blue Book revisions only have a small impact on the starting level of nominal GDP in our forecast – which is important as the denominator for fiscal aggregates – raising it by 0.1 per cent in the second quarter of 2026. There were also relatively limited changes to the composition of GDP. Given these modest revisions to the recent momentum in the economy, and to the starting level and composition of nominal GDP, it is unlikely that incorporating the Blue Book 2026 revisions would have had a material impact on our economy or fiscal forecasts.

Average hours and productivity

Productivity growth is one of the most important, but uncertain, judgements for our economy and fiscal forecasts. One of the sources of uncertainty relates to challenges in accurately measuring both the level of, and growth in, historical productivity.

Our preferred measure of productivity is output per hour worked.b To estimate total hours worked, the ONS previously used a ‘direct approach’ which drew on average hours worked reported in the Labour Force Survey. The ONS will now be moving towards a ‘component approach’ to calculate hourly productivity that draws on a wider range of data sources to construct estimates of labour input, in line with the latest international standards.c Revisions have, so far, been published on a whole-economy basis up to 2024. Previous OECD research finds that countries which rely on average hours taken directly from labour force surveys systematically overestimate labour input and so underestimate hourly productivity.d The ONS published this new approach after we had finalised the pre-measures forecast, so we did not reflect it in the forecast, and it will become the official estimate for hourly productivity from November.

Chart D shows that average hours worked are materially lower under this new methodology and have continued their pre-financial crisis downward trend, falling at an average annual rate of 0.3 per cent between 2010 and 2024. The difference between the direct and component approaches is largely explained by reducing the use of imputation when measuring the amount of annual leave taken, leading to higher measured annual leave and lower hours worked.e The revisions to average hours have not changed output or income, but have significantly changed the working hours required to produce that output.

Chart D: Average hours worked

Source: ONS

Table A shows that the reduction in labour input, combined with the slight upward revision to real GDP mentioned above, means that output per hour worked is now estimated to have grown at an average annual rate of 1.0 per cent between 2010 and 2024. This is close to double the previous estimate of 0.4 per cent a year and is just under the 1.1 per cent we expect productivity growth to rise to by the end of our forecast. The small change in the contribution to GDP growth from employment reflects revisions to the number of workers from taking on new data sources, which results in a small change in output per worker.

Table A: Contributions to GDP growth, 2010-2024

 GDPGDP per hourGDP per workerEmploymentAverage hours
 Percentage points

Difference(1)0.10.60.10.0-0.4

1) Difference in GDP is due to the component approach using updated quarterly national accounts data. Difference in employment is due to the wider range of data sources used to construct jobs estimate under the component approach.
Source: ONS, OBR

The revised data shows a significantly different picture of the slowdown in productivity growth following the global financial crisis. The slowdown still exists but to a lesser degree than previously thought and the new data make our forecasts over this period look less over-optimistic in some years (Chart E, left panel).f The new data also suggest that the UK’s productivity growth performance was closer to that of international comparators in the decade following the crisis, after previously appearing to underperform (Chart E, right panel).

Chart E: Output per hour, UK and the G7

Note: Chain-linked volume measures of GDP are used as the measure of output. UK figures are based on ONS data, whereas internationally comparable OECD figures are used for other G7 countries. Yellow bar in the right panel represents additional growth in output per hour from using the component approach.
Source: OECD, ONS, OBR

Ultimately, what matters for the outlook for potential output, GDP per person, average earnings and, consequently, for the fiscal forecast, is growth in output per worker – which combines forecasts for output per hour and average hours worked. And this set of ONS revisions has little effect on output per worker. After the data becomes the official measure of productivity in November, we will consider whether this, alongside any other developments and analysis, should result in any revisions to our next forecast.

a) See the Foreword for more details on our forecast timetable.

b) See Briefing paper No.9: Forecasting productivity for more information on how we forecast productivity and measurement issues.

c) See ONS, Component approach to measuring labour productivity, UK: 1997 to 2024, September 2026.

d) See OECD, International productivity gaps: Are labour input measures comparable?, December 2018.

e) This is largely driven by an explicit adjustment for annual leave that assumes workers fully utilise their holiday entitlements. While this is a strong assumption, which the ONS are continuing to investigate, they argue that it results in more reasonable estimates of annual leave than under the previous direct approach, which implied for several years that, on average, full-time employees took less annual leave than the statutory minimum. Several other adjustments, such as for sickness absences and parental leave, also lead to lower average hours worked and have remained broadly constant over time.

f) Productivity significantly increased during the Covid pandemic as restrictions led to a marked temporary decline in economy-wide average working hours, as well as low-productivity sectors being shut down and then reopened.

Output gap and real GDP

Output gap

2.47 Our swathe of output gap models, shown in Chart 2.11, points to a widening amount of spare capacity in the economy. The models are based on a variety of methods including statistical filters on GDP, extracting common trends from surveys of capacity utilisation, and structural filters using economic relationships with variables like inflation to make inferences about the output gap. Based on these and other indicators, our central estimate is that GDP is just over 1 per cent below its potential level at the start of the forecast. In the second half of this year, we expect higher energy prices to weigh on aggregate demand to a greater extent than supply, widening the output gap further. Relative to March, higher interest rate expectations also push down on our output gap forecast. As energy prices fall back, as implied by the market pricing that underpins our forecast, we expect a steady cyclical recovery in our central forecast, with the output gap closing by 2031.

Chart 2.11: Output gap

Source: OBR

Real GDP

2.48 Real GDP growth was robust around the start of the Middle East conflict, with quarterly growth of 0.6 per cent in the first quarter of 2026, but we expect higher energy prices to begin to weigh on growth from the second half of 2026. Our central forecast, shown in the left panel of Chart 2.12, is for real GDP growth of around 1¼ per cent in 2026, a slight improvement on our March forecast reflecting significantly higher-than-expected first-quarter growth.[11] In the second half of 2026 and into 2027, we expect higher global energy prices to feed through to the prices facing consumers and businesses, and higher interest rates to weigh on activity. We have therefore downgraded our central forecast for real GDP growth in 2027 to 1 per cent, 0.5 percentage points below our March forecast. As energy prices are then expected to fall back, our central forecast sees growth pick up to average 1¾ per cent a year over the rest of the forecast period. This leaves the level of GDP in 2030 broadly unchanged from our March forecast.

2.49 We judge that the risks around our central forecast are broadly balanced. The right panel of Chart 2.12 shows that, based on our historical forecast errors, there is a one-in-five chance that real GDP growth in 2027 could be higher than 2¼ per cent and a similar chance that real GDP contracts. In practice, the risks to the forecast will likely be different to those in the past. Upside risks to real GDP growth include a faster reduction in energy prices than we assume and stronger productivity growth, potentially driven by the wider adoption of AI. Downside risks include a larger or more persistent energy shock than implied by market pricing, increased geopolitical uncertainty, more disruption to trade due to uncertainty and the impact of tariffs, and weaker productivity growth. We explore the implications for growth of alternative energy price paths in Chapter 7. There is also a significant risk of wider unexpected shocks hitting the UK economy, with recent examples being the Covid pandemic and Russian invasion of Ukraine. These shocks tend to be larger on the downside than the upside and may result in large differences between forecast and outturn.

Chart 2.12: Real GDP

Source: ONS, OBR

2.50 Real GDP per person, an indicator of living standards, is 0.7 per cent higher in 2030 than in our March forecast. This is mainly explained by a higher starting level of potential output, with a small contribution over the forecast from higher growth in capital deepening. The lower forecast for net migration has very little impact on GDP per person as we judge that those net migrants would have had similar labour market characteristics to those of the general population, explained in paragraph 2.38.

Labour market

2.51 Labour market conditions continue to loosen as vacancies and the number of payrolled employees fall, while private sector pay growth has eased. Given the acknowledged issues with data quality within the LFS, we continue to draw on a wide range of evidence in our assessment of labour market conditions, including administrative data and surveys.[12] This is particularly the case for the participation rate and, consequently, employment growth. The left panel of Chart 2.13 illustrates that non-LFS measures of employment point to a subdued labour market, in contrast to the continued growth in LFS employment. Survey evidence, such as the latest Bank of England Decision Makers Panel, also points to broadly flat employment growth over the past year. HMRC administrative data suggest this continues to be driven by lower inflows into employment rather than higher outflows from employment. We also expect the Employment Rights Act 2025 (ERA) to weigh on the labour market, in addition to previously announced policies including the increases in employer NICs and the National Living Wage. See Chapter 3 for a detailed discussion of the impact of policy measures on the labour market.

2.52 The general trend in the LFS unemployment rate matches this picture of a loosening labour market, rising from its post-pandemic trough of 3.6 per cent in 2022 to 5.2 per cent in the final quarter of 2025 (right panel of Chart 2.13). While the unemployment rate in the LFS fell to 4.9 per cent in the second quarter of 2026, other evidence – such as the number of vacancies, the KPMG and REC UK Report on Jobs, and the employment component of the UK PMI – signal ongoing weakness in labour demand. Given these wider indicators, we expect the unemployment rate to rise marginally from its current level, peaking at just above 5 per cent in the second quarter of 2027, before falling to its estimated equilibrium rate of 4.1 per cent. The peak in unemployment is marginally lower than in our March forecast but comes a year later. We previously explored scenarios with alternative trajectories for the unemployment rate in our March 2026 EFO.

Chart 2.13: Unemployment rate and employment

Note: Administrative data is the HMRC RTI payrolls-based estimate from the Resolution Foundation. Workforce Jobs survey data, RTI employees data, and LFS data published on 15 September.

Source: ONS, OBR

Composition of economic activity

Households

Household saving rate

2.53 In our central forecast, the adjusted saving rate, excluding pension valuation effects, falls from around 5½ per cent in 2025 to just over ½ a per cent in 2031 (Chart 2.14). As described in paragraph 2.30, we are forecasting real household disposable incomes to fall slightly in the near term. In the face of this, we expect consumers to gradually reduce saving to smooth their consumption over time. There are significant risks around this forecast. For example, if higher interest rates or general economic uncertainty encourage higher precautionary saving, consumption would be weaker than in our forecast.

Chart 2.14: Household saving rate

Note: The ‘saving rate’ is defined as one minus the ratio of consumption to disposable income plus the change in net equity in pension reserves. The ‘adjusted saving rate’ is defined as one minus the ratio of consumption to disposable income.
Source: ONS, OBR

Household consumption

2.54 Real household consumption has stagnated since the pandemic, with its level in 2025 only 1 per cent higher than in 2019. We expect real household consumption growth to average ¾ of a per cent in 2026 and 2027, as weak real income growth weighs on spending growth. It then picks up to grow more in line with overall GDP in the medium term, following the recovery in real income growth. This leaves real consumption as a share of GDP at 61 per cent at the end of the forecast, over 2 percentage points below the average level from 2010-2019, and 0.4 percentage points below the level we forecast in March. The relatively weak growth in consumption reflects higher energy prices (and that these constitute a larger shock to real household disposable incomes than real GDP) and a higher share of consumption occurring via government-provided services since the pandemic.

Business sector

Profits

2.55 The rate of return on corporate capital and the profit share of GDP are both currently at low levels relative to recent history (Chart 2.15). After a period when firms have absorbed a range of cost increases into lower profits, including the rises in employer NICs and energy prices, we increasingly expect them to pass these costs on to households and rebuild their profit margins. Increasing real wages by slightly less than productivity growth should push up the rate of return and profit share towards more historically typical levels. The relatively loose labour market is likely to underpin this process by reducing workers’ bargaining power.

Chart 2.15: Rate of return and profit share of income

Note: The gross rate of return is calculated as private non-financial corporations’ (PNFC) gross operating surplus divided by their gross capital employed. PNFC gross operating surplus includes their gross trading profits and their rental income from residential and non-residential buildings, but excludes their inventory holding gains. Profit share in the right panel includes only PNFC gross trading profits.

Source: ONS, OBR

Business investment

2.56 Real business investment rose by around 2½ per cent over the first half of 2026, leaving its level 2 per cent above our March forecast by the second quarter of 2026. We expect some of this recent strength to unwind as headwinds from higher energy prices weigh on business activity and investment over 2027. We then expect growth in business investment to pick up, averaging 1¼ per cent a year. By 2030, we expect the level to be ½ a per cent above our March forecast, reflecting both its recent strength and a higher profits forecast.

Government

2.57 Real government spending, the sum of government consumption and investment, is expected to grow by just under 4 per cent in 2026, up from 2 per cent in 2025. Growth then slows to average 1¼ per cent over the remainder of the forecast. Weaker real departmental spending growth in the medium term leaves the level of total government spending around ½ a per cent lower in 2030 relative to our March forecast. Within government spending, consumption rises from 21 per cent of GDP in 2025 to 21¼ per cent in 2026. It then settles at around 21 per cent of GDP by the end of the forecast, significantly higher than the 19½ per cent in 2019. Government investment follows a similar profile, increasing from just under 3½ per cent of GDP in 2025 to nearly 4 per cent in 2027, before falling back to around 3½ per cent by 2031. This is around ¾ of a percentage point above the level in 2019.

Current account and sectoral balances

2.58 We forecast the current account deficit of the balance of payments to narrow from just over 3 per cent of GDP in 2025 to around 2½ per cent by 2030, as export growth outpaces import growth. Chart 2.16 shows how the matching inflow of foreign saving to fund this deficit represents a steady inflow of net lending from the rest of the world. As weak income growth drives a fall in household saving, we expect the household net lending position to fall from a surplus of around 1¾ per cent of GDP in 2026 to a deficit of 1¼ per cent by 2031. The corporate sector moves from a small deficit to a surplus by 2030 as firms rebuild the rate of return and investment falls slightly as a share of GDP. The public sector deficit narrows significantly over the forecast, but the profile remains similar to our March forecast.

Chart 2.16: Sectoral net lending

Note: Four-quarter rolling average.

Source: ONS, OBR

The housing market

Mortgage rates

2.59 We expect that the average effective interest rate on the outstanding stock of mortgages will rise from 4 per cent in 2026 to around 5 per cent in 2031. This is due to an upward-sloping yield curve pushing up quoted rates, and people rolling off mortgages that were fixed when rates were lower. As illustrated in the left panel of Chart 2.17, this effective rate is higher than the average since the financial crisis (3 per cent), but significantly lower than over the decades leading up to 2008. The stock of outstanding mortgage debt is around 85 per cent of household disposable income across the forecast period. This is lower than the peak during the financial crisis, but higher than at any time before the early 2000s, when effective mortgage rates were significantly higher. The right panel of Chart 2.17 shows that mortgage payments as a share of household disposable income are expected to rise to reach 4¼ per cent in 2031, the highest since 2009 and more than double the 2021 level of 2 per cent.

Chart 2.17: Mortgage rate and mortgage debt

Note: Shaded area marks forecast period. In the right panel, mortgage payments as a share of household disposable income are calculated by multiplying the stock of outstanding mortgages by the average effective mortgage rate.

Source: ONS, OBR

Housing supply

2.60 As shown in Chart 2.18, we expect net additions to the housing stock to fall from around 230,000 in 2025-26 to 220,000 in 2026-27 and 2027-28, before increasing gradually to 260,000 in 2030-31. Compared to the March forecast, we expect 190,000 fewer cumulative net additions over the forecast period. We expect the ’Your First Home‘ equity loan scheme to only have a marginal impact on housing supply given its limited overall size.

Chart 2.18: Net additions to the housing stock

Source: ONS, OBR

2.61 We have not changed our estimate from the March 2025 forecast that the National Planning Policy Framework (NPPF), on its own, would boost housebuilding significantly. But leading indicators, particularly housing permissions, suggest that housing supply will remain substantially weaker than would be consistent with our previous forecasts in the near term (see Chart 2.19). Over the medium term, we judge that the economic environment, including as a result of higher interest rates, and the wider regulatory picture, mean net housing additions are likely to rise more slowly than we previously expected:

  • Higher interest rates since late 2022, alongside other affordability pressures and weak effective demand, have weighed on the commercial viability of residential development. Industry evidence suggests that higher borrowing costs and weaker buyer sentiment have weighed on house price growth, reducing developers’ ability to absorb rising costs.[13] This points to a flatter near-term profile for net additions, with some medium-term recovery expected as some of these economic conditions improve.
  • The weaker profile for housing supply in the near and medium term also partly reflects the cumulative effect of regulatory and fiscal changes outside the planning system over the last decade, particularly since 2019. These policy developments have increased the cost, complexity, and timescales associated with residential development. These include measures aimed at improving building safety, such as the Building Safety Levy and Building Safety Regulator regime, environmental requirements such as Biodiversity Net Gain and nutrient mitigation schemes, and wider fiscal measures including the residential property developer tax and higher corporation tax rates. Taken together, these measures appear to have weighed on housebuilding activity to a greater extent than we had previously assumed.

Chart 2.19: Housing completions indicated by residential planning permissions

Note: The “Expected housing completions, given planning permissions outturn” line shows the path for housing completions implied by the latest outturn data for residential planning permissions. This is calculated by applying the historical average lag between permissions being granted and homes completed to the residential planning permissions outturn data. The latest residential planning permissions outturn data is from MHCLG analysis of Glenigan data and remains subject to revision.
Source: Glenigan data from 2016 to 2021 Q4, MHCLG analysis of Glenigan data from 2022 Q1 onwards, ONS, OBR

House prices and property transactions

2.62 We estimate that average house prices will grow by around 2 per cent in 2026 and by 2½ per cent in 2027. This would continue the recent trend of house price growth being below CPI inflation, largely driven by higher mortgage rates and stagnating real incomes. We then expect house price growth to average 2¾ per cent from 2028 onwards, as real income growth recovers. Compared to the March forecast, growth in house prices is therefore lower in the near term. The outlook for house prices remains highly sensitive to the path of mortgage rates, the evolution in real incomes, and the impact of planning and regulatory changes on housing supply. The ’Your First Home‘ scheme could marginally raise house prices by stimulating housing demand.

2.63 In our central forecast, property transactions grow from around 1.2 million in 2026 to around 1.3 million in 2031, as the housing market turnover rate returns to its assumed medium-term equilibrium that every house is transacted on average once every 24 years and as the housing stock continues to grow. The number of transactions across the forecast period is around 4 per cent lower than in the March forecast, as higher mortgage rates are expected to weigh on activity, and as we have revised down our forecast for growth in the housing stock.

Residential investment

2.64 Real residential investment (excluding transfer costs) is forecast to rise by 0.5 per cent in 2026, following weaker-than-expected outturns in the first half of the year, 0.2 percentage points lower than in our March forecast.[14] Growth then picks up to average around 3 per cent a year over the rest of the forecast, around 2 percentage points lower than in our March forecast. This leaves real residential investment 6¾ per cent lower in 2030 than in our March forecast. This weaker outlook is due to lower planned housing completions on new-build investment but is partially offset by diverted investment toward improving the existing stock.

Nominal GDP and its composition

2.65 We expect nominal GDP growth to slow from around 4 per cent in 2026-27 to 3¼ per cent in 2027-28, as growth in both real GDP and the GDP deflator slows. We then expect nominal GDP growth to average around 3¾ per cent a year over the rest of the forecast, as real GDP growth rises and inflation sits at around 2 per cent. Compared to the March forecast, cumulative growth in nominal GDP from 2025-26 to 2030-31 is 1¼ percentage points higher. This difference is driven almost entirely by higher growth in the GDP deflator. Whole-economy inflation is higher due to the second-round effects of the imported energy shock through wages and profits, and a rise in the price of energy produced in the UK.

2.66 This is reflected in stronger cumulative growth in all major components of nominal GDP, both in income and expenditure terms. On the income side, we expect strong growth in nominal corporate profits, a determinant of corporation tax receipts, averaging around 4½ per cent a year over the forecast period. Growth in nominal labour income, including wages and salaries and mixed incomes, averages just over 3 per cent a year over the forecast period. Labour income growth is a key judgement in the forecast given its comparatively high effective tax rate and that income tax thresholds are currently frozen. On the expenditure side, we expect nominal business investment growth to average around 2¼ per cent a year across the forecast, while nominal household consumption growth averages 3¾ per cent a year.

Comparison to external forecasters

2.67 Chart 2.20 compares our forecast for real GDP growth to a range of external forecasts. The interpretation of differences with external forecasters is more complex than usual. Ongoing volatility in financial and energy markets may obscure or exaggerate differences in underlying assumptions about the economic outlook, depending on when the forecast was constructed. Our real GDP forecast over the next two years is slightly lower than the latest short-term external forecasts (published in October), as it does not incorporate the stronger-than-expected GDP growth for July and August. From 2028, our forecast is at the higher end of the range of long-term external forecasts (published in August), with cumulative real GDP growth between 2025 and 2030 ¾ of a percentage point higher than the external average. Our forecast for real GDP growth, both cumulative and year-to-year, is similar to that of the Bank of England in its July Monetary Policy Report.

Chart 2.20: Real GDP: comparison of external forecasts

Note: Bank of England is from the July Monetary Policy Report. October external average uses the latest external forecasts for 2026 and 2027 published by HM Treasury in October 2026. The range and August external average are based on the latest five-year forecasts, published in August 2026.

Source: Bank of England, HM Treasury, OBR

2.68 As shown in Table 2.3, our forecast for CPI inflation next year is higher than both the Bank of England and the average of external forecasters. This difference is likely primarily driven by the timing of market determinants used in different forecasts,[15] the relative importance of import cost pressures for food and other tradable goods, and judgements about the degree of indirect and second-round effects. Our forecast for the unemployment rate is in line with the Bank of England and external forecasters over the next two years, but lower afterward. This is likely driven by different assumptions about the equilibrium rate of unemployment.[16]

Chapter 3: Policy measures

Introduction

3.1 The Charter for Budget Responsibility states that the responsibility for producing the costings of policy measures lies with the responsible policy department, which is typically HM Revenue and Customs for tax policies and the Department for Work and Pensions for welfare policies. These departments therefore hold the models and datasets that are used to produce these costings. The OBR’s role, as set out in the Charter, is to provide independent scrutiny and certification of these costings.[17] Requests for further information relating to the methodology and datasets used for costings should therefore initially be directed to the relevant department.

Total effect of Government decisions

The impact of fiscal policy decisions on borrowing

3.2 This forecast incorporates the fiscal implications of policy measures announced in this Budget and since the March forecast, which are summarised in Table 3.1 and Chart 3.1. The direct effect of these measures is to increase borrowing by £7.6 billion in 2026-27, £3.5 billion in 2027-28, and £0.4 billion in 2028-29. The direct effect of policy measures reduces borrowing thereafter by £4.1 billion in 2029-30, £5 billion in 2030-31 and £12 billion in 2031-32.

3.3 Policies that affect public spending are estimated to increase borrowing between 2026-27 and 2030-31 by an average of £3.3 billion, before planned spending reductions are estimated to reduce borrowing by £4.2 billion in 2031-32. This reflects:

  • Funding to support a £100 council tax rebate increases spending this year (2026-27) by £2.6 billion.
  • Funding for the defence investment plan is estimated to increase spending by an average of £1.8 billion a year. The valuation of unfunded public sector pensions increases spending by an average of £1.1 billion a year across the forecast.
  • Reductions in planned capital spending of £0.9 billion a year from 2027-28. The civil service transformation programme is estimated to reduce resource spending by £0.5 billion a year from 2028-29. In 2031-32, a reduction in the pre-measures spending assumption further reduces resource spending by £5.5 billion.

3.4 Changes to tax policy increase borrowing by £1.7 billion in 2026-27, before reducing borrowing by an average of £6 billion over the final four years of the forecast. This reflects:

  • The further freeze to fuel duty and the temporary zero-rating of VAT on domestic electricity reduce receipts in 2026-27 by £1.3 billion.
  • HMRC tax administration, compliance and debt collection measures raise an additional £3.6 billion by 2031-32. The largest of these measures is the introduction of timely payment for self assessment which is estimated to yield an average of £1.6 billion a year from 2029-30.
  • Changes to corporation and bank taxes are estimated to raise an average of £2.2 billion a year across the forecast. The reduction in writing down allowances is estimated to raise approximately £1 billion a year on average from 2027-28, while the temporary increase in the bank surcharge increases receipts by £1 billion in 2027-28.
  • Further increases to tax rates on savings and property income increase receipts by an average of £1 billion a year from 2028-29.

Chart 3.1: Direct borrowing impact of policy decisions included in this forecast

Note: A positive sign implies an increase in borrowing.

Source: HM Treasury, OBR

3.5 Table 3.1 also includes the indirect effects of policies on the public finances via any wider impacts they have on the economy. At this Budget, these indirect effects are relatively small and primarily driven by the impact that policy measures have on inflation (see paragraph 3.10) and by higher near-term borrowing increasing debt interest spending. These also include a £1 billion reduction in estimated departmental underspends against their day-to-day spending plans in 2027-28 due to the impact of spending policy in that year, discussed further in Chapter 5.

3.6 Across the forecast period, this policy package has a broadly neutral absolute average net impact (just 0.02 per cent of GDP, £1.3 billion). As shown in Chart 3.2, between 2010 and 2014 there were a series of events where policy was tightened overall in net terms. After this period, fiscal policy was loosened in net terms at most fiscal events, including at each event from March 2023 to October 2024. Since then, this Budget represents the fourth consecutive event where the absolute average net impact of planned policy measures on borrowing over the forecast has been less than or equal to 0.1 per cent of GDP.

3.7 However, this period includes events, such as Budget 2025, which saw significant near-term loosening followed by medium-term tightening. Chart 3.3 compares the profile of the overall fiscal impact of policy at this event, including indirect effects, against other policy events since June 2010. This shows that it follows a typical pattern of short-term policy loosening followed by medium-term planned policy tightening. The in-year policy loosening at this event is above the interquartile range of all other policy events for that year, while the planned tightening in the final two years is broadly in the middle of the range.
