Economic effects of policy measures

3.8 Our forecast explicitly accounts for the economic impacts of newly announced government policy where these are material. The impacts of policy on the supply side of the economy are explicitly accounted for in our forecast if credible evidence suggests the measures will have an additional, durable, and significant impact on potential output.[18] The Government has announced initial details of a range of policies since the March forecast – including at this Budget – which aim to raise GDP over the longer term. We will review the potential impact of such policies, over the medium- and long-term, when final details are confirmed and where sufficient evidence is available – including as part of our annual summer assessments of supply potential. Therefore, in this forecast, we have not made any explicit adjustments to potential output due to policy changes.[19]

3.9 Our assessment of the demand-side effects of fiscal policy is informed by ‘fiscal multipliers’. We define fiscal multipliers as the impact of a policy package on demand which, at least in the short run, can affect the output gap by changing the level of economic activity relative to potential output. Our standard multipliers are drawn from the empirical literature. However, there is no single multiplier that applies to all policies and at all points in the economic cycle. The multiplier may vary due to macroeconomic conditions and policy instruments. We therefore assess at each forecast what multipliers are appropriate.[20] We judge that the demand-side impact of this policy package is not sufficiently material to explicitly adjust our real GDP forecast. The overall package is relatively small in fiscal terms and much of the initial fiscal loosening has been pre-announced. Additionally, forward-looking agents may smooth economic activity through the initial fiscal loosening and subsequent fiscal tightening arising from policy.

3.10 The changes to fuel duty and the temporary cut to VAT on domestic electricity bills have small effects on our inflation forecast. They reduce CPI inflation by 0.08 percentage points in 2026-27 and 0.02 percentage points in 2027-28, before raising CPI inflation by 0.04 percentage points in 2028-29. On a quarterly basis, at its peak, these policies reduce CPI inflation by 0.22 percentage points in the second quarter of 2027 and increase CPI inflation by 0.16 percentage points in the second quarter of 2028.[21],[22]

Spending measures included in this forecast

Changes to departmental expenditure

3.11 Changes to departmental spending increase borrowing by £5 billion in 2026-27 and £1.2 billion in 2027-28, before reducing borrowing by £6 billion in 2031-32. We discuss changes to departmental spending in greater detail in Chapter 5. The largest changes are:

  • a £100 council tax rebate for properties in bands A to D in England which increases borrowing by £2.6 billion in 2026-27;
  • the defence investment plan, which increases departmental resource spending (RDEL) by £1.8 billion a year on average;
  • a £5.5 billion reduction to the RDEL pre-measures spending assumption in 2031-32; and
  • changes to departmental capital spending, which reduce borrowing by an average of £0.9 billion a year from 2027-28.

Other spending policies

3.12 Other policies contributing to the changes in spending in this forecast include:

  • The valuation of public service pension schemes and consequent reduction in employer contributions, which increases net spending on unfunded pension schemes by an average of £17 billion per year in the medium-term. The Government has decided to reduce departmental resource spending in line with the lower cost of employer contributions, by an average £16 billion from 2027-28 onwards. The overall net effect of these changes is therefore to increase overall spending by an average of £1.1 billion from 2027-28. This net cost arises from the participation of employers in public service pension schemes whose staff costs are not funded through departmental spending, so the lower employer contributions are not offset. This policy is discussed in greater detail in Chapter 5.
  • A package of further measures to address fraud and error in the welfare system is expected to reduce welfare spending by £0.5 billion in 2031-32. This includes the hiring of 2,000 verification assurance officers, 200 additional staff to prevent fraud and error from those living abroad, and 150 additional staff to reduce capital-related fraud and error in pension credit. We discuss the staffing and departmental spending implications of these measures in more detail in paragraphs 3.14 and 3.15.

Receipts measures included in this forecast

Tax administration, compliance, and debt collection measures

3.13 The Government has announced further HMRC tax administration, compliance, and debt collection measures in this Budget which are estimated to raise an additional £3.6 billion by 2031-32. These measures are targeted at the current high levels of tax debt and reducing the overall tax gap through increased HMRC compliance activity, and include:

  • The introduction of timely payment for self assessment, and part reversal of the Budget 2025 measure that mandated coding out via the PAYE system, which brings more payments of self-assessment liabilities forward into the tax year in which income is earned. This measure is estimated to yield an average of £1.6 billion a year from 2029-30 which reflects the timely payment system preventing some debt losses and changing the timing of recovery of debt. The yield is adjusted to account for self-assessment customers reducing their forecast liabilities and additional delayed debt created due to payment pressures in the transition year. Further information on the non-debt cash timing impacts of the measure is detailed in paragraph 3.25.
  • Changes to HMRC’s compliance workforce which are estimated to yield an additional £0.4 billion in 2031-32. This accelerates the recruitment of the previously announced 5,500 additional HMRC compliance officers and funds an additional 670 staff by the end of 2029-30. The recruitment of these compliance officers will be equivalent to a 23 per cent increase to HMRC’s compliance workforce compared to March 2024. The expected yield has been adjusted to account for diminishing returns of additional compliance officers, where marginal yield from additional activity is likely to be below the average yield from business-as-usual compliance activity, disruption impacts, and optimism bias to reflect uncertainty around recruitment, performance of staff, and nature of future non-compliance activity.
  • Other measures which yield £1.4 billion in 2031-32, and include extending HMRC’s powers to directly recover outstanding tax debts; mandating the use of e-invoicing to reduce opportunities for error and other forms of non-compliance; investing to place part of the established stock and future flows of outstanding tax debt with debt collection agencies; and extending the VAT online marketplace liability rules to UK businesses, with a minimum platform threshold at £90,000. The interactions between measures targeted at debt collection have been captured by adjusting the tax bases to capture the reduced revenue available to collect as a result of measures targeting the same customer group or types of debt.

Box 3.1: Compliance policy and the tax gap

In our scrutiny of tax compliance policies, in addition to considering the impact of each measure individually, we also consider the overall impact of the full package of measures on the projected ‘tax gap’. This is the difference between the amount of tax that should, in theory, be paid and the amount received, and so is a measure of overall non-compliance. While most tax liabilities are paid voluntarily through the normal operation of the tax system, other liabilities are only identified or recovered through HMRC compliance activity. HMRC records the additional revenue generated, and losses prevented through this activity as ‘compliance yield’. Other things equal, higher yield from compliance and debt collection activities should reduce the amount of tax that ultimately remains unpaid and, in turn, the tax gap.

HMRC’s latest estimate of the tax gap was £59 billion or 6.4 per cent of total theoretical liabilities in 2024-25 (left-hand side, Chart A).a These estimates suggest the tax gap has risen since the mid-2010s, following a period of decline, although the increase appears to have flattened in recent years.b As shown on the right-hand side of Chart A, small businesses have accounted for a growing share of the tax gap in recent years, rising from around 40 per cent of the total in 2017-18 to more than 60 per cent since 2021-22. Of the compliance measures announced in this Budget, a third of the yield is expected to reduce the tax gap, with the residual reflecting either debt acceleration or payments. Of the 15 measures expected to reduce the tax gap announced this Budget, four target the small business tax gap.

Chart A: Tax gap: recent trends and forecast

Note: Customer group splits are produced by HMRC, we have simplified these by combining the series for ‘individuals’ and ‘wealthy’.

Source: HMRC, OBR

Recent fiscal events have included large numbers of compliance and debt measures aimed at closing the tax gap. Chart B shows the expected yield from all compliance and debt measures announced at fiscal events since 2014.c Since the start of 2024, around 65 new measures have been announced, which together are estimated to reduce the tax gap by around £8 billion in 2029-30, with just over half of this driven by measures announced at Autumn Budget 2024. This increase in estimated yield from compliance and debt measures in the forecast period partially drives the forecasted decline in the tax gap shown on the left-hand side of Chart A.

Chart B: Estimated yield from compliance and debt measures announced since Spring 2014 impacting the tax gap

Note: This chart reflects when announced yield is estimated to impact the tax gap. Yield estimates shown here are ‘debt management adjusted’ and therefore differ from total yield. Debt management yield that changes the timing of payments, rather than the amount of tax ultimately collected, is excluded, while yield that reduces amounts otherwise lost through write-offs or remissions is included. HMRC estimates the latter using the age of debt in scope of each measure and the associated estimated impairment rate. The non-adjusted total compliance yield expected from these policies in 2029-30 is around £12 billion.

Source: HMRC, OBR

Estimates of the yield from compliance measures are highly uncertain. This reflects uncertainty around how taxpayers will respond to interventions and around the effectiveness of the operational delivery of such measures. To account for this, we apply a range of adjustments including attrition, which reflects how behavioural responses can erode yield over time, and optimism bias, to reflect the risk that evidence or delivery assumptions overstate yield. These adjustments are intended to produce a central estimate, but the resulting yield will nonetheless remain uncertain.

Looking top-down, the historical and projected future path of the tax gap indicates both upside and downside risk to the forecast. The forecast assumes a significant and sustained decline in the tax gap following a period across which it has been broadly flat. However, there have been previous periods of steeper declines in the tax gap, most significantly in the early 2010s. Given these risks, we will continue to monitor and evaluate the tax gap outturn compared to the estimated yield from compliance and debt measures, and, as set out in the article we published in October 2025, will undertake additional work with HMRC to further improve the approach used to produce these costings, including standardising the bottom-up assumptions we use to arrive at a central estimate of the yield.d

a) HM Revenue and Customs, Measuring tax gaps 2026 edition: tax gap estimates for 2024 to 2025, June 2026. There is a lag in the tax gap publication data and as such the most recent, 2026, estimates cover the 2024-25 financial year.

b) HMRC cautions against interpreting changes in tax gap estimates over time as evidence of underlying trends, given the impact of data revisions, methodological changes and uncertainty around the estimates themselves.

c) This is an illustrative comparison between scored yield and outturn and projected tax gap. Left-hand side of Chart A shows the expected impact of measures on the tax gap which have been re-costed at subsequent events, whereas Chart B shows the original scored yield from compliance and debt measures announced at fiscal events. Both have been adjusted to only capture yield from debt measures which has an impact on the tax gap.

d) Turner, A., and B. Garrett, Estimating the yield from tax compliance policies, 2025.

3.14 These measures aimed at addressing the compliance tax gap and fraud and error in the welfare system will require additional DEL funding across the forecast period. As RDEL settlements for HMRC and DWP have not been agreed beyond 2028-29, we requested additional information from the Government to ensure the activity captured by these measures would be truly additional to HMRC and DWP baseline activity. We also asked the Treasury to provide assurances that HMRC and DWP would receive the funding necessary to achieve the baseline compliance and fraud and error activity implicit in our forecast. The funding provision for baseline HMRC and DWP activity and measures announced at this Budget and Budget 2025 is set out in Table 3.2.

3.15 The Treasury has also agreed, once the full funding allocated to HMRC and DWP has been finalised in the next Spending Review, to undertake a review of the estimated yield from compliance and fraud and error measures announced since Autumn Budget 2024 which will be implemented beyond the current Spending Review period. We will also review the approach to post-Spending Review period measures in future as part of our ongoing work on the compliance tax gap and fraud and error in the welfare system.

Changes to corporation and bank taxes

3.16 The Budget includes several changes to corporation and bank taxes, which increase receipts by £2.3 billion in 2031-32. These include:

  • A reduction to the writing down allowance (WDA) main rate from 14 to 12 per cent, alongside an increase in the new first-year allowance introduced in January 2026 from 40 to 50 per cent from April 2027.[23] This is estimated to raise an average of £1 billion per year from 2027-28.
  • A temporary 3 percentage point increase to the bank surcharge, from 3 to 6 per cent, effective from 1 January 2027 to 31 March 2028. Over the 15 months this is in effect, it is estimated to yield a total of £1.5 billion. This is net of an estimated £190 million reduction in receipts[24] as some banks are assumed to move profits out of the UK in response to the higher tax rate. The size of this effect is uncertain.
  • Restricting sovereign wealth and overseas public pension funds’ immunity from direct taxes on UK income and gains from January 2029 raises £0.7 billion by 2029-30. This estimated yield includes a behavioural effect which significantly reduces the costing to reflect the affected population restructuring their assets, increasing leverage, and an attrition adjustment. This costing is deemed highly uncertain because of a lack of administrative data on the tax and assets of the affected group, and uncertainty surrounding the degree to which these groups will respond to minimise the impact of these changes.

Increase to tax rates on savings and property income

3.17 This Budget further increases the tax rates on savings and property income. These rates were already set to increase by 2 percentage points in April 2027 following measures announced at Budget 2025. From April 2027, the basic, higher, and additional rates will increase by a further 2 percentage points to 24, 44, and 49 per cent respectively. Together, the measures announced at this Budget are estimated to raise £1.0 billion by 2031-32:

  • The additional 2 percentage point increase in all rates of saving income tax is estimated to yield £0.6 billion a year on average from 2028-29. A behavioural response, primarily reflecting additional usage of other tax advantaged assets, such as Individual Savings Accounts, reduces the yield by around £0.2 billion a year.
  • The further 2 percentage point increase in all rates of property income tax is estimated to raise £0.4 billion a year on average from 2028-29. The costing of this policy includes small behavioural responses, reflecting additional tax-motivated incorporation and higher non-compliance, that reduce the yield by £0.1 billion a year. The potential long-term impact of the increase to property income tax rates and other related measures is discussed in paragraph 3.46.

Changes to dividend and capital gains taxation

3.18 The Budget includes the following changes to the dividend and capital gains tax (CGT) regimes:

  • Changes to the taxation framework for distributions from companies to individuals from April 2028 which is estimated to raise £0.8 billion by 2029-30. This introduces a number of changes to the system which aim to prevent company owners paying tax at lower CGT rates rather than at dividend income tax rates. The costing includes a significant reduction in the estimated yield over time to reflect the targeted tax base’s ability to plan to reduce the additional tax liability.
  • A reduction to the lifetime limit for the business asset disposal relief (BADR) from £1 million to £500,000 from April 2027 increases receipts by £0.3 billion in 2029-30. The introduction of a 10 per cent CGT rate for qualifying founders of high-growth or innovative companies from April 2027 reduces receipts in 2029-30 by £0.3 billion.

Other changes to taxes

3.19 This forecast also incorporates other changes to taxes which raise £1.1 billion in 2026-27, £1.3 billion in 2027-28, before falling to £0.2 billion by 2031-32. These include:

  • The Government has announced the abolition of the carbon price support (CPS) from April 2028 and provided funding for the Low Carbon Contracts Company (LCCC) enabling it to reduce contracts for difference (CfD) levies on billpayers. These measures cost an average £0.4 billion a year from 2027-28 onwards.[25] These measures are intended to offset the increase in electricity bills for non-energy-intensive-industry consumers due to the British Industrial Competitiveness Scheme (BICS) and an uplift to the Supercharger scheme.
  • In Budget 2025, fuel duty rates were frozen and the reversal of the 5p fuel duty cut delayed until September 2026. The Government has since announced a further freeze to fuel duty rates and delay of the reversal until January 2027, with this Budget extending these until June 2027. According to the latest policy, on 1 July 2027 the temporary five pence cut – first introduced in 2022 – will be reversed in full alongside an uprating of fuel duty rates. If implemented, it would lead to fuel duty rates rising from 52.95 pence per litre to 60.59 pence per litre. This policy costs £0.6 billion in 2026-27 and £0.8 billion in 2027-28. The reversal of the 5p cut has now been delayed five times and fuel duty has not been uprated with RPI since 2011. Paragraph 3.43 and Chapter 7 sets out the fiscal impacts if this latest iteration of fuel duty policy were to again not be implemented.
  • A cut to VAT on domestic electricity from 5 per cent to 0 per cent until April 2027 announced in July, with this Budget extending the cut until July 2027. This is expected to cost £0.7 billion in 2026-27 and £0.3 billion in 2027-28.
  • The Government has announced a package of temporary business rates measures through the remainder of the current valuation period, at a net cost of around £0.5 billion in 2028-29. These measures include a real-terms freeze in business rates liabilities for properties in certain sectors, increased reliefs for pubs and live music venues, and higher eligibility thresholds for small business rates relief. The Budget also extends existing schemes that limit increases in business rates bills following revaluation. The cost of these measures is partly offset by a two-year extension of the 1p transitional relief supplement.
  • The Budget also incorporates mayoral strategic authorities into the business rates retention system on a permanent basis. This increases the share of net business rates revenues flowing into local government by approximately £0.5 billion in 2031-32.[26] The measure means that a larger share of future growth in business rates revenues accrues to mayoral authorities rather than central government. This policy forms part of the Government’s wider devolution roadmap, which we discuss further in paragraph 3.38.

Measures impacting the Government’s balance sheet

3.20 The Government has increased the funding available for the National Wealth Fund and the British Business Bank (BBB) to invest in financial assets, such as loans and equity. This raises the Government’s financing requirement by £1.9 billion a year on average, and increases debt by £3.9 billion in 2031-32. The new investments are estimated to reduce borrowing by £0.5 billion in 2031-32, reflecting £0.6 billion of additional income, which is only slightly offset by expected losses due to loan write-offs and calls on guarantees. Taken together, the net impact of these measures is to increase debt by £3.4 billion in 2031-32.

3.21 The Government has also announced additional capacity for UK Export Finance (UKEF) to provide guarantees to back the purchases of British exports by foreign buyers. UKEF expects to issue a total of £40 billion of additional guarantees evenly between 2027-28 and 2030-31. This reduces borrowing by £0.2 billion a year over the medium-term but materially increases the public sector’s exposure to geopolitical risk. The £7 billion expansion of the Growth Guarantee Scheme offered by the BBB is expected to increase borrowing by £0.1 billion in 2026-27 and 2027-28 and £0.2 billion thereafter, reflecting the expected calls on the guarantees, which outweigh the fee income earned.

3.22 The ‘Your First Home’ Equity Loan Scheme will provide loans to first-time buyers of newbuild homes totalling £0.7 billion in 2027-28 and of £1 billion from 2028-29 onwards. This increases debt by £0.7 billion in the first year, thereafter the annual increase in debt from new loans is increasingly offset by loan repayments. We expect the scheme to only have a marginal impact on house prices and supply given its limited overall size.

Measures with highly uncertain costings

3.23 We assign an uncertainty rating to all certified policy costings. The measures that we have given a ‘high’ or ‘very high’ uncertainty ratings are set out in Table 3.3.

3.24 The only highly uncertain measure not discussed above is the measure increasing the Company Share Option Plan (CSOP) scheme individual limit and removing the interaction with Enterprise Management Incentives so individuals can benefit from both allowances fully. There are high levels of uncertainty around the level of additional take-up of these schemes and the extent to which additional take-up maximises the increased options allowances.

Classification treatment of policies

3.25 On the advice of Treasury classification experts and pending an ONS decision on classification this section sets out how we are recording the following new Budget transactions:

  • Fees levied on landlords to register with local authorities are recorded as central government taxes and so increase receipts, while fees on developers to participate in the equity loans scheme are recorded as sales of goods and services and so decrease spending. These decisions do not affect borrowing or debt.
  • The introduction of timely payment for self assessment means that affected taxpayers make payments towards both their previous and current year liabilities during the transition year of 2029-30. These overlapping payments, together with the part reversal of the Budget 2025 measure that mandated coding out via the PAYE system, increase cash receipts in 2029-30 by £17 billion. As explained further in Chapter 6 the additional cash receipts reduce the CGNCR and public sector net debt in 2029-30, as both are measured in cash terms. We assume that the ONS will make an extraordinary accruals adjustment in 2029-30 to remove the temporary cash timing effect from these overlapping payments but will not do so in subsequent years.

3.26 The Treasury has also decided to switch the British Business Bank and National Housing Bank from departmental expenditure limits (DEL) to annually managed expenditure (AME).

Update on previous measures

3.27 We cannot review and re-cost all previous measures at each fiscal event, but we do look at those where the original (or revised) costings seem to be under- or over-performing, and costings that were identified as particularly uncertain.

3.28 The yield from the Budget 2025 measure to no longer exempt salary-sacrificed pension contributions from NICs, above an annual £2,000 threshold, has been revised up by an average of £0.4 billion in 2029-30 and 2030-31. This is driven by outturn data suggesting higher usage and average contributions than previously assumed and a stronger nominal earnings forecast.

3.29 In response to underperformance in the delivery model of their targeted case review (TCR) regime, DWP has decided to insource 2,500 FTE staff who were previously working for a third-party contractor to boost their productivity, as well as to hire an additional 900 FTE staff to undertake more case reviews. We included AME savings from this operational expansion in our pre-measures forecast. Despite the Government’s intention for these additional 900 staff to be permanent, we were informed after the closure of our pre-measures forecast that they would not be funding these staff beyond 2028-29. We will review both the resourcing and AME savings of this measure at our next forecast.

Update on previous indirect effects judgements

3.30 Together with the Treasury and departments responsible for implementation of the various policy measures, we have established transparent processes for monitoring and evaluation of the supply-side impacts of policies on potential output. We use these processes to check whether observed outcomes are consistent with the assumptions underpinning our economy forecast. We have made no explicit adjustments to the forecast as a result of monitoring and evaluation at this event. For larger policies, there is not yet evidence that their effects differ materially from those incorporated in our forecast, while policies experiencing delivery issues have relatively small effects in the forecast.

3.31 As set out in Chapter 2, in this forecast we have revised down our projection for new housing supply over the forecast period by 190,000. We have not, however, at this point revised our previous estimates of the positive contribution that the National Planning Policy Framework (NPPF) reforms would make to housing supply over this period. Instead, our assessment is related to the wider regulatory and fiscal environment and to sharp rises in construction costs. This illustrates the challenges associated with assessing the economic effects of regulatory policies, where the full range of relevant regulatory changes affecting any specific sector can be difficult to identify and quantify.[27] We will keep our NPPF assumption under review. A feasibility study for the evaluation of the NPPF reforms is expected from MHCLG in early 2027.

3.32 We have previously not included an assessment of the potential economic impacts of the Employment Rights Act 2025 (ERA), given it was dependent on ongoing consultations and secondary legislation. We have made an assessment at this event, following the conclusion of most of the consultations and the implementation of various components of the reforms. Our analysis suggests that the ERA will likely have a negative economic impact from negative impacts on businesses, particularly through increased uncertainty, lost flexibility and higher labour related costs, which will more than offset any potential positive impact on worker productivity.

3.33 However, the size of the impact of the ERA is highly uncertain, partly due to the difficulties mentioned above in assessing the economic effects of regulatory policy. It is likely that the negative impact from the final set of reforms is substantially less than the initial policy proposals put forward in 2024 would have been. There is also still some uncertainty about the final policy design of the zero-hour contract related measures. However, overall, we judge there is currently not clear evidence that this negative impact is above our 0.1 per cent potential output threshold, so we have not made an explicit adjustment to our forecast. We will keep this judgement under review.

3.34 The latest evidence on the increase in employer NICs announced at Autumn Budget 2024 does not suggest that its impacts have been materially different from our expectations. Sharper falls in employment among lower-paid sectors are consistent with our judgement that these firms would be most affected by the reduction of the secondary threshold and its interaction with the National Living Wage. That said, it remains too early to draw firm conclusions about the scale of the policy’s impacts relative to our original assumptions, given the wider effects impacting the labour market (see Chapter 2). A full evaluation is expected from HMRC in 2027.

3.35 At previous events we have incorporated estimates of the increase to labour supply from government employment support programmes. The extension to Talking Therapies, which we estimated would increase employment by 10,000 in our November 2023 forecast, was intended to increase both the number of completed treatments and the average number of sessions. However, this extension has not been delivered. Connect to Work, which we assumed – in our 2023 forecasts – would increase employment by around 25,000, aimed to help people with health conditions and disabilities into employment. The number of participants supported is now expected to be around a third lower than we assumed by 2028-29. These examples illustrate the challenges of making explicit adjustments to the economy forecast for departmental spending policies, given that the impacts are highly sensitive to subsequent changes to policy and operational design, possible reprioritisation of funding, delivery challenges, and wider changes to spending programmes which may have offsetting effects.[28]

3.36 The latest evidence on the expansion of 30 hours free childcare, suggests take-up has been stronger than expected. This was announced at Spring Budget 2023 and, as of June 2026, the number of active users of the offer was 17 per cent above the DfE target. This has increased spending on the policy relative to original expectations. However, higher take-up does not necessarily imply a larger labour supply effect – we originally estimated that this would increase employment by 60,000. Some parents receiving the additional support may have worked similar hours in its absence – particularly those on higher incomes – and it is possible that individuals earning above the £100k cut-off threshold have reduced their hours worked to receive the childcare benefit. The DfE evaluation for this policy is expected in 2027.

Policy risks

3.37 Our legislation requires that our forecasts reflect only current government policy. As such, when the Government sets out ‘policy ambitions’ or ‘intentions’, we ask the Treasury to confirm whether they represent firm policy. We use that information to determine what should be reflected in our forecast. Where they are not yet firm policy, we note them as a source of risk to our central forecast. A full database of risks to this forecast and the changes from previous updates will be available on our website.

3.38 The Government’s devolution roadmap sets out initial details of the transfer of powers and resources in England. This includes policies for local retention of income tax receipts from April 2028 and discretionary power for mayoral and foundation strategic authorities to introduce an Overnight Visitor Levy. We will incorporate any material economic and fiscal impacts of these reforms into our forecast once the detailed policy and implementation arrangements have been finalised.

3.39 The Timms Review of Personal Independence Payment and Milburn Report on youth, health, and employment have both published interim findings with final recommendations expected later this year. We will assess the economic and fiscal impacts of any resulting policy changes once the Government has responded and the policy details are sufficiently clear.

3.40 The Government has announced several future spending commitments following the end of the current Spending Review period. As discussed in paragraphs 5.16 to 5.19, this includes the Government’s commitment to reach 3.5 per cent of GDP spent on defence by 2035 and a commitment to significantly increase council house building. We will incorporate the economic and fiscal impacts of the final announcements made in these areas after the next Spending Review.

3.41 The Government has announced its intention to establish a National Care Service in England and has brought forward the Casey Commission’s recommendation on its design to 2027. The fiscal cost of these reforms is highly dependent on the final design of the reforms and the timing of their introduction. For example, the Health Foundation has previously estimated that reforms to social care could increase spending by between £3.5 billion and £17 billion a year, depending on the generosity and design of the system.[29] We will incorporate the economic and fiscal impacts once the detailed policy and funding arrangements have been confirmed. Based on the current policy regime, we projected in the 2026 Fiscal risks and sustainability report that adult care spending in the UK would rise as a share of GDP from 1.2 per cent to 1.8 per cent over the next 50 years, largely reflecting demographic pressures associated with the UK’s projected growing old-age population. These spending pressures are expected to be most pronounced in the 2030s as the baby-boom cohort reaches their 80s, given the concentration of adult social care costs towards the end of life.

Extensions of temporary policies

3.42 The Budget includes several extensions to policies that are described as temporary and therefore not included in the forecast beyond the short term. This continues a pattern of such extensions at successive fiscal events. This introduces risk into the medium-term forecast which is produced on the basis of these temporary policies expiring.

3.43 At this Budget, fuel duty rates have again been frozen for a further 10 months until July 2027, from when the ‘temporary’ 5p cut to rates were first introduced at Spring Statement 2022.[30] The cumulative cost of freezing fuel duty rates between 2010-11 and 2027-28 and the 5p cut, relative to the stated policy that they will increase in line with RPI inflation, has risen to £139 billion. As shown in Table 7.1, if fuel duty were to remain frozen until 2029-30, public sector net borrowing would be £4.3 billion higher.

3.44 Other smaller measures which have been extended through repeated short-term policy decisions include various reliefs for business rates, the universal credit surplus earnings limit – which has been extended for the tenth consecutive year – and tariff suspension extensions.

The long-run impact of government policy

3.45 The Charter for Budget Responsibility requires us to assess the long-term impacts of new government policies, where these are material. We therefore report on new policies with long-run economic and fiscal impacts, especially where these differ from the effects that are expected to have occurred by the five-year timeframe at which we are required to produce our medium-term forecasts.

The long-run economic impact of measures

3.46 The 2 percentage point increase in property income tax rates in this Budget will be implemented on top of the 2 percentage point increase announced at Budget 2025. Both of these measures will come into force from April 2027 and represent a material reduction in returns to private landlords. This follows various measures over the past 10 years that have also reduced private landlord returns.[31] These measures collectively risk leading to a steady rise in rents through two effects. First, potential landlords are less likely to enter the market, reducing long-run rental supply, which risks a steady long-term rise in rents if demand outstrips supply – which we think is more likely than not. Second, landlords are likely to pass on more of these costs to renters in the long-run through a combination of some current landlords leaving, thereby giving more price setting power to remaining landlords, and greater long-run flexibility to raise rents. Such rises in rents would offset at least some of the reduction in the commercial viability to landlords from the measures introduced in recent years.

3.47 The India-UK Free Trade Agreement could increase real GDP by amounts rising to 0.13 per cent by 2040, according to the Government’s impact assessment.[32] As these economic benefits are expected to build up gradually over the long-term, we do not make an explicit adjustment in the medium-term forecast. The UK is also currently negotiating with the EU on various policies, including sanitary and phytosanitary measures and alignment between the UK Emissions Trading Scheme and EU Emissions Trading System. The economic impact will depend on the outcome of these negotiations.

The long-run fiscal impact of measures

3.48 In our Fiscal risks and sustainability reports (FRS), we have highlighted the long-term costs of the triple lock policy which, under its current design, uprates the state pension each year by the greater of average earnings growth, CPI inflation, or 2.5 per cent. In the 2026 FRS long-term scenarios, an ageing population – combined with the triple lock – drove a significant projected increase in state pension spending from 5 per cent of GDP in 2025-26 to 9 per cent of GDP in 2075-76. We estimated that the triple lock accounted for around one third of this increase. In the FRS baseline long-term scenario, which assumes pensions are uprated by the triple lock, public sector debt is projected to reach 300 per cent of GDP by 2075. In a scenario where the state pension is instead assumed to be uprated by earnings each year, debt was projected to reach 260 per cent of GDP by 2075.

3.49 Under the adjusted triple lock system, which is expected to take effect from April 2030, the state pension will be uprated by the highest of CPI inflation, 2.5 per cent, or the increase required to maintain its value relative to earnings at 2029-30 levels. As a result, if the value of the state pension moves above its value relative to earnings in 2029-30 (due to periods in which inflation or 2.5 per cent is higher than earnings growth) then in subsequent periods (where earnings growth is higher than inflation or 2.5 per cent) it would converge back toward this relative value. This removes the so-called spending ratchet effect under the current triple lock whereby increases in pension values relative to earnings are permanently retained and compounded over time.

3.50 We have incorporated the adjusted triple lock into our central medium-term forecast from 2030-31 but it does not currently affect state pension costs over the forecast period.[33] However, the adjusted triple lock is likely to materially reduce the growth of state pension spending over the long term relative to the current triple lock. The exact long-term impact of this change will be highly dependent on the future relationship between earnings growth, inflation, and the 2.5 per cent floor. To illustrate this, Chart 3.4 applies earnings uprating, the current triple lock and the adjusted triple lock to historical outturn data for earnings and inflation since 1990. This shows that:

  • 1990-2007: Average earnings growth was typically above both inflation and 2.5 per cent.[34] As a result, the illustrative pension values under both the current triple lock and adjusted triple lock would have remained very close to those produced by earnings uprating alone.
  • 2008-2014: Inflation or 2.5 per cent consistently exceeded earnings growth. As such, pensions under both the current and adjusted triple lock would have been uprated by the same amount each year and seen their value relative to earnings rise.
  • 2015-2025: Earnings growth exceeded inflation or 2.5 per cent over seven of the 10 years. These are the years in which the current and adjusted triple lock mechanisms would have produced different pension value outcomes:
  1. under the current triple lock, pensions would be uprated by earnings growth in these years, maintaining the gaps with earnings indexation that develop when inflation or the 2.5 per cent targets bind. This illustrates the ratchet effect of the current triple lock, under which this gap can only widen over time.
  2. under the adjusted triple lock, pensions would instead have been uprated by inflation or 2.5 per cent in these years, narrowing the gap with earnings indexation. In a scenario where earnings continued to exceed inflation and 2.5 per cent in subsequent years, this gap would eventually close under the adjusted triple lock.

3.51 Over the period from 1990 to 2025, the adjusted triple lock would have produced annual outcomes relative to earnings growth and the current triple lock that varied significantly across the different economic environments described. Averaging over the period as a whole, state pension payments would have been on average 1.4 per cent lower each year than under the current triple lock and 4.4 per cent higher than under earnings uprating.

3.52 This analysis illustrates that while the adjusted triple lock will not eliminate the long-term cost of state pension uprating relative to earnings indexation, it is expected to reduce costs relative to the current triple lock. Provisional analysis based on the historical experience described above suggests that if the next 50 years looked similar to the last 35 years on average, the adjusted triple lock would reduce the projected long-term increase in debt by around 25 per cent of GDP in 2075-76 relative to retaining the current triple lock.[35] Debt would be around 10 per cent of GDP higher in the long-term than under an earnings uprating scenario, reflecting the likelihood that there would continue to be periods where the inflation or 2.5 per cent uprating mechanisms would bind.

Chart 3.4: The value of the state pension under alternative uprating mechanisms

Note: The above reflects illustrative analysis of how pension values would have evolved under alternative uprating mechanisms between 1990 and 2025. It assumes that the adjusted triple lock would be conditioned on maintaining pension value relative to earnings at 1990 levels and that these alternative mechanisms would have bound across all years shown.

Source: OBR

3.53 This is an illustrative estimate of the long-term fiscal impact of the adjusted triple lock. These estimates depend critically on the order, level and covariation of wage growth and inflation over time. We intend to develop our long-term modelling approach ahead of the next FRS so that we can provide a fuller analysis of the adjusted triple lock mechanism and assess its sensitivity to different long-run economic assumptions.

3.54 These initial illustrative estimates suggest that the adjusted triple lock is likely to materially reduce future spending pressures relative to current policy. However, alongside the announced adjustment to the triple lock, the Government stated its intention to expand state provision of social care. The overall impact of these announcements on the long-term public finances therefore depends on the additional cost of the social care reform which, as discussed in paragraph 3.41, depends on the final policy design and the timing of its introduction.

Chapter 4: Public sector receipts

Summary of the receipts forecast

4.1 In the central forecast, total public sector receipts are projected to rise as a share of GDP from 40.1 per cent of GDP (£1.2 trillion) in 2025-26 to 42.9 per cent of GDP (£1.6 trillion) in 2031-32. Within this, as Chart 4.1 shows, National Accounts taxes as a share of GDP (the ‘tax take’) are forecast to increase sharply from 36.0 per cent of GDP in 2025-26 to 37.9 per cent of GDP in 2027-28, surpassing the previous historical high, before then rising more gradually to reach a peak of 38.6 per cent of GDP at the end of the forecast period.[36] This would be a 5.7 per cent of GDP increase on the pre-pandemic level of 32.9 per cent of GDP in 2019-20.

4.2 Table 4.1 details the main drivers of the expected 2.6 per cent of GDP increase in the tax take from 2025-26 to 2031-32. Increases in personal taxes (income tax and National Insurance contributions) contribute 1.6 per cent of GDP to this increase, reflecting forecast earnings growth, frozen personal tax thresholds until April 2031, and policy measures that raise self-assessment receipts. Capital taxes contribute 0.5 per cent of GDP to the increase due to projected rises in equity prices and the expected impact of inheritance tax and capital gains tax (CGT) policy changes largely announced in Autumn Budget 2024. Onshore corporation tax contributes a further 0.4 per cent of GDP to the increase mainly due to strong expected near-term profits growth.

4.3 Relative to our March 2026 forecast, National Accounts taxes as a share of GDP are forecast to be 0.2 per cent of GDP higher this year and just 0.1 per cent of GDP higher in 2030-31, as higher forecast receipts in nominal terms are almost offset by higher forecast nominal GDP. Of the rise in nominal receipts, around four-fifths of the increase in 2030-31 compared to March is due to changes in the underlying forecast, primarily due to a stronger nominal earnings forecast and higher forecast asset prices and profits. Around one-fifth is accounted for by the tax policy changes announced in this Budget.

Source: ONS, OBR

4.4 Chart 4.2 shows the contributions to the change in the National Accounts tax-to-GDP ratio, which is forecast to be 5.7 percentage points higher in 2031-32 than it was in 2019-20, just before the Covid pandemic. They are:

  • 2019-20 to 2025-26: Nearly all major taxes grew as a share of GDP during this period, adding 3.1 percentage points to the tax-to-GDP ratio by 2025-26. The contribution of income tax rose sharply after 2022-23 due to the freezing of personal tax thresholds combined with the subsequent period of high inflation. NICs receipts fell in 2024-25 due to the reductions to employee and self-employed NICs announced in Autumn Statement 2023 and Spring Budget 2024, but then rose again in 2025-26 due to the employer NICs rises announced in Autumn Budget 2024. The rise in onshore corporation tax was driven by the increase in the main rate from 19 per cent to 25 per cent in April 2023 and resilient profits in higher tax-paying sectors. Oil and gas receipts rose sharply in 2022-23 due to high energy prices and the introduction of the energy profits levy, then fell back as prices declined. Fuel duty was the only major tax to see a persistent fall as a share of GDP after the pandemic because of successive duty rate freezes, the 5p rate cut announced in Spring Statement 2022, and the increasing use of electric vehicles.
  • 2025-26 to 2031-32: The further forecast increase in the tax take of 2.6 per cent of GDP over this period is mainly driven by further expected growth in personal taxes, capital taxes and onshore corporation tax, as described in paragraph 4.2. This is expected to be slightly offset by further declines in fuel duty receipts, alongside reductions in receipts from alcohol and tobacco duties. Much of the increase is expected to take place by 2027-28, which is also temporarily boosted compared to 2028-29 by expected one-off receipts from the reforms to the previous non-domiciled tax regime, with more gradual increases then expected from 2028-29 onwards.

Chart 4.2: Contributions to the change in the tax-to-GDP ratio since 2019-20

Source: ONS, OBR

4.5 Chart 4.3 shows the same projected 5.7 percentage point rise in the tax-to-GDP ratio from 2019-20 to 2031-32 decomposed into the impact of forecast and policy changes:

  • The inherited tax system adds 0.8 percentage points to the tax-to-GDP ratio over this period. This is the increase in tax revenues that is driven by the structure of the tax system in place at the time of the pre-pandemic Budget in March 2020, combined with the expected evolution of the economy over the period to 2031-32.
  • Underlying forecast changes, including outturn and economy forecast changes, add an expected additional 0.7 percentage points, which is mainly due to higher forecast profits and earnings.
  • The direct impacts of government policy decisions from Spring Budget 2020 to Spring Budget 2024 increase the tax-to-GDP ratio by a further 1.7 percentage points between 2019-20 and 2031-32, primarily through freezes to personal tax thresholds and the increased headline rate of corporation tax.
  • The direct impacts of Government policy decisions from Autumn Budget 2024 onwards add a further 2.5 percentage points to the tax-to-GDP ratio between 2024-25 and 2031-32, including through the measures announced at this Budget that increase it (by 0.2 percentage points).

Chart 4.3: Contributions to the rise in the tax-to-GDP ratio from 2019-20 to 2031-32

Note: ‘October 2024 and onwards’ incorporates Government policy decisions that were made between March and October 2024.

Source: ONS, OBR

4.6 Chart 4.4 illustrates the sensitivity of the receipts forecast to some of the key drivers of expected receipts growth using ready reckoners. The chart shows the impact of assuming that the level of average earnings, nominal consumption and equity prices are higher or lower than our central forecast by their median absolute forecast difference in each year.[37] By 2031-32, this would mean the levels of nominal earnings and consumption, two of the largest tax bases, are around 6 per cent higher or lower than in the central forecast, and equity prices around 10 per cent higher or lower. Additionally, we assume in the downside sensitivity that the tax gap remains flat as a share of GDP from 2024-25,[38] and in the upside sensitivity that it falls as a share of GDP by double the rate in our central forecast.

4.7 The left panel shows the overall impact on public sector receipts of these upside and downside sensitivities relative to the central forecast, and the right panel decomposes this impact by economic and fiscal variables:

  • In the upside sensitivity, receipts are higher by £82 billion (2.2 per cent of GDP) in 2031-32.[39] The largest driver is the impact of higher nominal earnings on income tax and NICs, boosting receipts by £50 billion in 2031-32, while higher equity prices increase capital taxes, onshore corporation tax, self-assessed income tax and interest and dividend receipts by £12 billion. Higher nominal consumption increases VAT receipts by around £10 billion in 2031-32. The narrowing of the tax gap raises receipts by around £10 billion.
  • In the downside analysis, receipts are lower by a symmetric amount, reducing receipts by £82 billion (2.2 per cent of GDP) in the final year.

Chart 4.4: Sensitivity of receipts to key economic drivers and the tax gap

Source: HMRC, ONS, OBR

Changes in receipts since the March 2026 forecast

4.8 Table 4.2 shows that in the central forecast, receipts in nominal terms are expected to be £12 billion higher in 2026-27 than in the March 2026 forecast, with the difference rising to £20 billion in 2030-31. This is primarily driven by:

  • Underlying forecast differences, which increase receipts by £14 billion by 2030-31, due to stronger-than-expected in-year receipts outturn and stronger forecast growth in profits, nominal earnings and equity prices (discussed further in paragraph 4.9 below);
  • PSNB-neutral forecast differences, which are offset in spending, including changes to depreciation, VAT refunds and environmental levies, which increase receipts by £1.4 billion in 2030-31;
  • The direct effect of Government decisions announced since the March 2026 forecast including in this Budget, which reduces receipts by £1.7 billion in 2026-27 but increases receipts by £6.6 billion in 2030-31. This is mostly due to near-term tax reductions on VAT on domestic electricity and business rates, and further delays to the rise in the fuel duty rate, that are later offset by measures boosting self-assessed income tax, including the modernisation of the distributions framework, and the increase to savings and property income tax rates;
  • The indirect effects of Government decisions announced in this Budget, which lower receipts by £2.1 billion in 2030-31, partly due to a small negative effect on nominal GDP including from some measures reducing the GDP deflator.

4.9 Chart 4.5 shows changes in the nominal receipts forecast compared to March 2026, split into the contributions due to changes to the pre-measures economy forecast, receipts outturn data, and policy changes. Higher forecasts for nominal profits and nominal earnings are the largest drivers – increasing pre-measures receipts by £10 billion and £9 billion, respectively, in 2030-31 compared to the March forecast. Other economic drivers include stronger-than-expected equity price growth which raises the forecast by £3 billion in 2030-31, and slightly weaker expected employee numbers which reduce it by an almost equivalent amount. Stronger-than-expected PAYE income tax and NICs outturn receipts so far this year increase the forecast by £4.2 billion in 2031-32, while weaker outturn for stamp duty land tax reduces it by £3.3 billion.

Chart 4.5: Receipts: drivers of the change since March

Source: OBR

Note: ‘Pre-measures economy forecast’ refers to changes in economy variables before the impact of policy, discussed further in Chapter 2. ‘Outturn’ refers to the difference against our March 2026 forecast in the final outturn data included in our pre-measures forecast as set out in the Foreword.

4.10 HMRC cash outturn receipts for 2026-27, received up to August 2026 which is the final outturn data included in our pre-measures forecast, were £3 billion above the profile consistent with our March forecast for 2026-27. The latest data up to September 2026 – received after closing the pre-measures forecast – show this has increased to £7 billion against the March profile. This is likely to primarily reflect the stronger-than-expected growth in nominal earnings and profits so far this year.[40]

4.11 In this forecast we expect public sector current receipts for the full year of 2026-27 to be £12 billion higher than forecast in March, which remains broadly consistent with the latest September cash receipts. Income tax (excluding self-assessed), NICs and onshore corporation tax receipts are expected to remain stronger than in our March forecast through the rest of the year as is the case in the September data. As outlined in Box 4.1, oil and gas revenues, which have been lower than expected up to August, are expected to recover in the remainder of the year due to the higher oil and gas prices. CGT receipts, mainly received in January 2027, are expected to be £5 billion higher than expected in March, reflecting an updated assessment of the strength in receipts seen in 2025-26 (paragraph 4.33). However, CGT receipts are typically volatile from year to year, so this is a particularly uncertain part of the in-year forecast.

4.12 Overall receipts in 2026-27 are expected to be relatively backloaded, with relatively more receipts expected in the final three months of 2026-27 than in a usual year. This is partly due to expected receipts from the Temporary Repatriation Facility introduced alongside reforms to the previous non-domiciled tax regime. As we set out when these reforms were announced, the estimated yield from this policy is highly uncertain.

Tax-by-tax analysis

Income tax (excluding self-assessed) and National Insurance contributions

4.13 Income tax (excluding self-assessed) and National Insurance contributions (NICs) are forecast to raise £520 billion (16.2 per cent of GDP) in 2026-27, a 7.6 per cent increase from 2025-26. This rise is driven by strong nominal earnings growth combining with frozen tax thresholds. Receipts are then forecast to rise to £610 billion (16.5 per cent of GDP) in 2030-31, before remaining broadly flat as a share of GDP in 2031-32 as thresholds return to being uprated by CPI from April 2031. Almost all of this growth comes from higher PAYE income tax because it is a more progressive tax than NICs, and is therefore boosted by the freezes to tax thresholds. A further temporary increase is expected to income tax receipts between 2026-27 and 2028-29 due to the Temporary Repatriation Facility announced as part of previous reforms to the non-domiciled regime.

4.14 Budget 2025 extended the allowances and threshold freezes in income tax and NICs to April 2031, meaning the majority will be frozen for most of the 2020s. This increases the sensitivity of the receipts forecast to nominal earnings changes through ‘fiscal drag’. Using our standard ready reckoners, a 1 percentage point increase in nominal earnings and CPI would raise income tax and NICs receipts by an average of £7 billion a year under the current policy of frozen thresholds to April 2031. This is around £2.5 billion a year higher than if thresholds were increasing with CPI, reflecting the additional fiscal drag generated.[41] This is also likely to have an adverse effect on financial work incentives by increasing the marginal tax rate on each additional hour of work. Following our standard approach, we have estimated that, taken together, the threshold freezes in income tax and NICs from 2022-23 to 2030-31 could decrease labour supply by 215,000 in average-hours equivalent (AHE) terms by 2030-31.[42]

4.15 Table 4.3 shows that relative to the March forecast, non-SA income tax and NICs receipts are expected to be £9 billion higher in 2026-27. This reflects the higher-than-expected PAYE receipts received so far this year, which has likely been driven by nominal earnings slowing by less than expected compared with the March forecast. Nominal earnings growth has been particularly strong in the public sector, in part due to the earlier timing of pay awards compared with previous years that may have boosted receipts against our previously assumed profile for receipts in 2026-27. Over the rest of the forecast, receipts are expected to be £8 billion higher, on average, than expected in March. This is driven by the higher outturn this year and a higher forecast for nominal earnings growth next year, which are only partly offset by a weaker forecast for the number of employees and the effects of Government decisions in 2029-30 and 2030-31.

4.16 The main effect of Budget policy decisions is through the measure to introduce timely payment for self-assessment and part reversal of the Budget 2025 measure that mandated coding out via the PAYE system, explained in more detail in paragraph 3.13. This transfers receipts from PAYE to SA IT that were previously associated with the coding out measure. The indirect effects of policy decisions have a very small downward impact on receipts in most years.

Self-assessed income tax

4.17 Self-assessed income tax (SA IT) is forecast to raise £63 billion in 2026-27 (2.0 per cent of GDP), a 17.2 per cent increase on 2025-26. This sharp increase compared to 2025-26 is driven by the previously announced reforms to the non-domiciled tax regime, which are expected to generate additional receipts from this year onwards, the personal tax threshold freezes, and forecast growth in self-employment and dividend income. Receipts are then forecast to rise to £94 billion (2.5 per cent of GDP) in 2031-32, driven by further expected receipts from the non-domiciled tax regime reforms, the reclassification of carried interest into SA IT from CGT, and policy measures announced at this Budget.

4.18 Table 4.4 shows that compared to March, SA IT receipts are £1.0 billion lower in 2026-27 but higher thereafter by increasing amounts, peaking at £11 billion in 2030-31. The pre-measures forecast changes reflect a stronger profits forecast boosting dividend income, and the impact of higher equity prices on the carried interest forecast. The main effect of Budget policy is through the measure to introduce timely payment for self-assessment. This is estimated to increase accrued SA IT receipts by £3.5 billion in 2029-30 and £6.5 billion in 2030-31. This is partly offset by reductions in PAYE IT receipts, outlined above, which lower the total net yield of the measure by around two-thirds in each year. This measure is expected to increase cash SA IT receipts by around £21 billion in 2029-30, only partly offset by a reduction of around £5 billion in PAYE IT cash receipts. Paragraph 3.25 provides further detail on the accounting treatment of this measure. The modernisation of the distributions framework is estimated to increase SA IT receipts by around £2 billion in 2029-30 and 2030-31, partially offset by the expected reduction in CGT receipts from this measure. The increase to savings and property tax rates is estimated to increase SA IT receipts by an average of £0.8 billion a year from 2028-29.

VAT

4.19 VAT receipts are forecast to raise £186 billion in 2026-27 (5.9 per cent of GDP), a 3.3 per cent increase from 2025-26. Receipts are then expected to rise to £230 billion by 2031-32 (6.0 per cent of GDP). The modest rise in VAT as a share of GDP over the forecast reflects the assumption that non-compliance will reduce over the forecast period, and that temporary VAT policies such as the zero-rating of VAT on domestic electricity will end.

4.20 As shown in Table 4.5, in the pre-measures forecast, receipts have been revised down by £1.2 billion in 2026-27 compared to March. Around a third of this is from an adjustment to reflect the September 2025 Tribunal verdict that locum doctors are VAT exempt and can reclaim overpayments backdated up to four years. Another third reflects reduced receipts due to some private hire vehicle operators (PHVOs) changing their business models outside London since the Budget 2025 decision to charge VAT on PHVOs. Over the rest of the forecast, pre-measures VAT receipts are lower in each year compared to March and are forecast to be £2.9 billion lower by 2030-31, largely due to a reduction in the proportion of goods and services assumed to be paying the standard rate of VAT.

4.21 The direct effect of policy measures announced at this Budget initially reduce VAT receipts by £1 billion over 2026-27, largely from temporary zero-rating of VAT on domestic electricity. From 2028-29 onwards, Budget measures increase VAT receipts by £1.1 billion each year. Of this increase, £0.8 billion is from new measures to increase VAT paid by PHVOs and locum doctors, and the extension of VAT online marketplace liability rules, while the rest is largely from measures aimed at reducing non-compliance and collecting tax debt. Further details are provided in Chapter 3. The indirect effects of Budget policies lower VAT receipts in each year.

Onshore corporation tax

4.22 Onshore corporation tax is expected to raise £107 billion (3.4 per cent of GDP) in 2026-27, an increase of 11 per cent from 2025-26. This increase is driven by higher profits in both the non-financial and financial sectors. Receipts are then forecast to rise to £136 billion (3.6 per cent of GDP) by 2031-32. This is mainly driven by the forecast for profits as a share of the economy increasing slightly over the forecast period.

4.23 Table 4.6 shows that relative to March, onshore corporation tax is expected to be £5.8 billion higher in 2026-27 with higher receipts from the financial sector explaining around three-quarters of the upward revision. This reflects higher-than-anticipated profits from both retail and investment banks so far this year and a delay in the effect on receipts from the Financial Conduct Authority’s motor finance compensation scheme, which raises expected receipts by £1.5 billion in 2026-27 and reduces receipts by £1.2 billion in 2027-28.

4.24 Receipts are expected to be £8 billion higher in 2030-31 than forecast in March. The pre-measures forecast is £5.7 billion higher in 2030-31 due to higher receipts this year and a higher forecast recovery in the profit share relative to March (discussed further in paragraph 2.55). This is only partially offset by decreases to the forecast due to the net effect from company tax data updates and modelling changes to estimates of group relief and capital allowances. Budget policies overall increase receipts by £2.3 billion in 2030-31. This includes £1.1 billion from a reduction in the main rate of writing down allowances, £0.7 billion from restrictions to sovereign wealth funds’ and overseas public pension funds’ immunity from direct tax, and £0.5 billion from tighter rules around companies’ ability to use foreign branch losses against UK profits. Further information on these measures is provided in Chapter 3. The indirect effects of Budget policies lower receipts in each year.

4.25 Receipts from the bank surcharge, not included in Table 4.6, are expected to raise £2.4 billion in 2027-28. This is an increase of £1.2 billion from 2025-26 and is £1.1 billion higher than expected in the March forecast. This reflects the announcement at this Budget that the rate of the surcharge will increase from 3 per cent to 6 per cent from January 2027 until March 2028. Receipts are then forecast to fall back to £1.6 billion by 2031-32.

Oil and gas receipts

4.26 Offshore corporation tax, petroleum revenue tax (PRT), and the energy profits levy (EPL) are expected to raise £5.1 billion (0.2 per cent of GDP) in 2026-27, a 25 per cent increase on 2025-26 due to higher oil and gas prices. Oil and gas receipts are then expected to fall over the remainder of the forecast, as the recent rises in energy prices are expected to unwind, down to £1.9 billion by 2028-29. Receipts are then forecast to decline further to £0.3 billion in 2030-31 and close to zero in 2031-32, primarily due to the expiry of the EPL in September 2029 and the expected continued fall in oil and gas production.

4.27 These forecasts are based on the North Sea Transition Authority’s (NSTA) projection for total oil and gross gas production, shown in Chart 4.6. This shows that production has been declining since the start of the 2000s and that this decline is expected to continue at around 10 per cent a year over 2027 to 2031. The projection includes production from currently producing fields (including Jackdaw), and the NSTA’s estimate of production from development of new fields, which includes Rosebank, and from future discoveries.[43] The forecast annual pace of decline would be around 14 per cent a year if there were no production from any new fields.

Chart 4.6: UK oil and gas production

Source: NSTA, OBR

4.28 Relative to our March forecast, oil and gas receipts are expected to be £2.6 billion higher in 2026-27, largely due to significantly higher oil and gas prices. However, the increase has been partly offset by the latest assessment of the impact on receipts of mergers and acquisitions (M&A) activity, described in Box 4.1. Receipts from the energy profits levy are now expected to be £9 billion in total between 2026-27 and 2030-31, up from £2.5 billion at the March forecast. The EPL is now expected to remain in place until September 2029, when the Energy Security Investment Mechanism (ESIM) is expected to trigger based on anticipated average oil and gas prices. This is two years later than in the March forecast.

Box 4.1: The impact of mergers and acquisitions on oil and gas revenues

Recent merger activity by firms operating in the UK continental shelf has resulted in companies with a large stock of tax losses combining operations with tax-paying companies, allowing greater use of losses against taxable profits. The 2021 to 2023 period saw expansion of larger and mid-sized oil and gas companies, outside of the major international groups. Since 2024, several of these companies have merged with portfolios held by major international companies.

The impact of this activity was a key factor behind the downward revision to our November 2025 forecast for offshore corporation taxa and in this forecast has muted the upward impact on receipts this year of higher oil and gas prices. Our tax forecasts are generated by HMRC’s North Sea microsimulation model, which captures M&A activity by combining information on company ownership of fields and tax returns data. The mergers in 2025 and 2026 had a similar pattern of dampening the receipts forecasts in both this and the November 2025 forecast, largely through making the stock of historical losses more readily accessible to profitable companies.

The left panel of Chart A shows the impact on losses used of the mergers incorporated at this forecast. The blue bars show the change in losses used due to recently completed mergers, which increase by a net £2.7 billion between 2026 and 2031. The frontloaded pattern reflects the assumption that, once historical losses become accessible, they are used as quickly as possible. Higher oil and gas prices in the early part of the forecast also generate more profits against which the losses can be used. This frontloading of loss utilisation results in a faster rundown of the stock of outstanding losses, leaving fewer losses available for use in the later years of the forecast.

The forecast also includes an assumption that a further 20 per cent of the stock of losses become accessible from possible future mergers.b The impact of this, shown by the yellow bars, is to increase losses used by £2.2 billion between 2026 and 2031.

The right panel shows the impact on receipts from the greater losses used, which mirrors this pattern, with greater reductions in the early years of the forecast when more losses are available against taxable profits. The net impact between 2026-27 and 2031-32 is a reduction of £1.3 billion from completed mergers and £0.8 billion from the adjustment for future mergers.

Chart A: Impact of M&A activity on losses used and oil and gas receipts

Note: The charts reflect impacts to ring fence corporation tax and the supplementary charge.

Source: HMRC, NSTA, ONS, OBR

a) The affected taxes are ring fence corporation tax (RFCT), the supplementary charge (SC) and petroleum revenue tax (PRT). The energy profits levy does not allow the use of historical losses from the RFCT, SC, and PRT regimes. See the ‘Oil and gas revenues’ Forecast in-depth page on our website for more detail on these taxes.

b) This is a high-level judgment and is not linked to any specific future transactions or groups.

Road taxes

4.29 Fuel duty is expected to raise £24 billion (0.8 per cent of GDP) in 2026-27, which is £0.6 billion less than in 2025-26, largely due to higher pump prices reducing demand. Relative to the March forecast, receipts are expected to be £0.7 billion lower this year mostly due to policy changes and lower demand. In May 2026, the Government announced that fuel duty rates would remain unchanged until January 2027. In this Budget, the Government has announced that rates will remain unchanged until July 2027 at which point the 5p cut first implemented in March 2022 will be unwound and then uprated by RPI. Receipts are then forecast to increase by £1.8 billion (7 per cent) in 2027-28 and by £0.9 billion in 2028-29 assuming this policy is implemented.

4.30 On the assumption that the latest policy is implemented, fuel duty receipts are forecast to peak in nominal terms at £27 billion (0.8 per cent of GDP) in 2028-29 before falling by around £2.1 billion by 2031-32. This reflects the turning point when the additional revenue from the assumed increase in fuel duty rates is forecast to be outweighed by the declining tax base due to a rising electric vehicle (EV) share. Chart 4.7 shows that in practice fuel duty revenues have been consistently lower than forecast. This is largely because despite successive policy commitments to raise fuel duty rates, they have not actually been increased since 2011-12. If the duty rate were to again remain unchanged at its current level throughout the forecast period it would reduce receipts, on average, by £4 billion a year between 2027-28 and 2031-32.

Chart 4.7: Successive fuel duty forecasts and outturn

Source: ONS, OBR

4.31 Vehicle excise duty (VED) receipts are expected to be £9 billion (0.3 per cent of GDP) in 2026-27, a 6 per cent increase compared to 2025-26 driven by an increase in vehicle registrations. Across the forecast period, receipts are expected to increase in each successive year, rising to £14 billion by the forecast horizon. The introduction of the electric vehicle excise duty (eVED) from April 2028 adds over £2 billion to VED receipts by 2031-32. Relative to the March forecast, VED receipts are expected to be lower by £0.2 billion on average across the forecast following weaker stock and outturn updates for non-EVs. This has been slightly offset by higher eVED receipts from higher fuel prices inducing faster-than-expected switches to EVs.

Capital taxes

4.32 Capital gains tax (CGT) is forecast to raise £26 billion (0.8 per cent of GDP) in 2026-27, around a 5 per cent increase from 2025-26. Receipts are then forecast to rise to £41 billion in 2031-32 (1.1 per cent of GDP), largely driven by a forecast of rising equity prices.

4.33 Relative to the March forecast, before the impact of policy measures, CGT receipts are expected to be £4.8 billion higher this year largely due to a revised assessment of the forestalling of receipts in 2025-26, which is explained below.[44] Thereafter, CGT receipts are forecast to be higher than the March forecast by an average of £3.1 billion each year, driven by the 2025-26 receipts reassessment and the impact of higher forecast equity prices. The Budget 2026 policy to reform the taxation framework for distributions from companies to individuals is estimated to reduce CGT receipts by £1.2 billion in 2031-32. However, this is more than offset by the expected increase in SA income tax receipts from this measure. The net impact of the new Founders’ Relief and the lower Business Asset Disposal Relief lifetime limit reduce receipts by an average of £0.1 billion a year from 2027-28.

4.34 CGT receipts for 2025-26, which largely relate to 2024-25 asset disposals, were 78 per cent higher than the previous year. This was partly due to forestalling – an increase in the disposal of assets in 2024-25 ahead of anticipated CGT policy rate increases at Autumn Budget 2024. In the March 2026 forecast, we made a judgement that £4.3 billion of liabilities were brought forward due to this forestalling. This assumption was based on the average magnitude of forestalling seen ahead of anticipated policy changes before Budget 2021 and ahead of rate changes in 2010. We assumed that £1.1 billion of these forestalled liabilities related to transactions that would have taken place later in that same year, resulting in a net £3.2 billion of additional liabilities in the 2024-25 tax year.

4.35 HMRC have since reviewed this forestalling assumption using a more detailed analysis of the pattern of asset disposals across the year.[45] Compared with a ‘normal’ CGT tax year, the distribution of disposals in 2024-25 suggests that around £5.8 billion of CGT liabilities were brought forward due to forestalling. The analysis also suggests that a higher proportion of these forestalled liabilities than previously assumed were likely to have been brought forward from later in that year (£4.3 billion), because the pattern of disposals later in the year was relatively weak compared to previous years. This suggests there was a net £1.5 billion of additional disposed liabilities in the 2024-25 tax year due to forestalling. The larger in-year unwinding increases the estimate for structural disposals in 2024-25. This, alongside the subsequent lower unwinding, boosts the forecast for disposals and receipts in the next three years compared to March.

4.36 CGT is one of the most volatile and therefore uncertain tax streams. This is reflected in the average size of the difference between CGT forecasts and outturn. Over the last ten forecasts, the one-year ahead CGT average absolute forecast difference was 24.5 per cent of forecast, compared with 4.3 per cent for total public sector current receipts.[46] Revenues are sensitive to movements in asset prices, in particular equity prices (see Box 3.1 in the March 2026 EFO), which are typically highly volatile from year to year. In addition, receipts are highly sensitive to behavioural decisions made by a relatively narrow group of higher net worth taxpayers, who often have flexibility in choosing when to dispose of assets in response to changes in asset value or to policy changes.
