(1) There is some evidence that the UK OIS curve we use to condition our forecast for Bank Rate may be less risk-free than usual. For example, see Lengyel, A. and D. Walker, Bank Rate expectations in the UK curve following the war in Iran, Bank of England Insights, July 2026. As is set out in Forecasts in-depth: Conditioning assumptions on our website, we condition our forecasts on the expected values implicit in market pricing. Since these reflect the collective views of a large number of investors in each market, it is unlikely that a forecast could systematically outperform these values.

(2) We recently published more information on our CPI inflation modelling framework, in line with the recommendations from our 2025 external review to share more transparently our analytical infrastructure. See Cavallero, J., The OBR’s CPI inflation modelling, OBR articles, August 2026.

(3) External forecasts based on more recent readings of energy market pricing suggest that the Ofgem energy price cap could rise to around £2,150 in Q1 2027. This figure represents the simple average of estimates from the forecasting services of EDF, British Gas, and E.ON Next available as of 21 October 2026.

(4) See Bank of England, Decision Maker Panel survey, September 2026.

(5) RHDI measures the purchasing power of households’ monetary income. It does not account for changes in household wealth due to asset prices, changes in the quantity and quality of the provision of goods and services by the Government, the state of the environment, or factors that may change quality of life such as leisure time. For details, see ONS, Understanding real household disposable income per head, November 2025.

(6) Current LFS data are based on an older vintage of National Population Projections (NPPs), which assumed a higher level of net migration. Since March, we have taken on the April 2026 ONS NPPs, which imply higher inflows into the adult population and marginally fewer deaths than the previous NPPs. As such, LFS outturn for the adult population in the second quarter of 2026 was 145,000 higher than we expected in March.

(7) We recently published more information on how we forecast net migration, including our migration model, in line with the recommendations from our 2025 external review to share more transparently our analytical infrastructure. See Robson, E., Forecasting net migration, OBR articles, September 2026.

(8) For example, migrants arriving on work visas tend to have a higher participation rate than the domestic population, while dependants and migrants on humanitarian visas tend to have lower participation rates.

(9) We have updated the model described in Box 3.2 of our 2022 FRS in line with the latest data available at the time of finalising our pre-measures forecast for the intermediate consumption of fossil fuels in the UK economy.

(10) We use sectoral GVA published on 30 September divided by sectoral employment. Sectoral employment is calculated using RTI employees and WFJ self-employment (both published on 15 September), scaled to align to the total aggregate employment measure constructed by the Resolution Foundation. Output per hour worked is our preferred measure of labour productivity, but limitations with the LFS make it challenging to examine trends at a sectoral level. Output per worker is also less likely to be significantly impacted by the change to the new ‘component’ approach discussed in Box 2.2.

(11) Although in recent experience real GDP growth has tended to be stronger in the first half of the year, ONS analysis has not found evidence of residual seasonality in quarterly or monthly GDP data. See ONS, How the ONS assesses statistical outputs for residual seasonality, May 2026.

(12) This is in line with an ONS recommendation to use the LFS as part of a suite of labour market indicators. See ONS, Labour market overview, UK, September 2026.

(13) The RICS national sales-to-stock ratio has fallen since late 2022, indicating weaker demand relative to the stock of properties available for sale. At the same time, residential construction costs have risen more rapidly than house prices over this period, putting further pressure on development viability.

(14) Transfer costs are additional costs associated with buying housing, such as stamp duty land tax and estate agents’ and solicitors’ fees.

(15) Our latest economic forecast is conditioned on the average of market determinants over the 10 working days to 19 August. The central projection in the Bank of England’s July Monetary Policy Report is conditioned over the 15 working days to 20 July.

(16) There are conceptual differences in how the OBR and the Bank of England consider spare capacity and equilibrium in the economy. See Box 3.1 in the March 2014 EFO.

(17) We have certified the costings for all but one measure in this forecast. Small changes were made to the policy parameters of the fuel duty policy announced in the Budget following the costings certification deadline. As a result, we could not certify the final version of the relevant costing but have used the Government’s estimates in this forecast and will finalise the certification for our next forecast. We do not expect this will have a material impact on the costing we have used.

(18) We define ‘significant’ as when a measure, or package of measures, changes the level of potential output by at least 0.1 per cent by the fifth year of our forecast. See Briefing Paper No. 10: Accounting for the supply-side effects of policy.

(19) We have not included an assessment of changes to visa salary requirements announced at this Budget, as the final policy was confirmed after the deadline for including it in the economy forecast. We will consider the impacts of these changes in our next forecast.

(20) See Chapter 2 in our June 2026 Forecast evaluation report.

(21) The fuel duty policy confirmed in the Budget is slightly different to that reflected in our economy forecast as the Government informed us of the final policy after the relevant deadline. Incorporating the final policy would have had less than a 0.1 percentage point impact on our quarterly inflation forecast.

(22) While other policy measures may also impact CPI inflation, their impacts are not material enough to warrant an explicit adjustment to our inflation forecast.

(23) The measure applies to both corporation tax and income tax populations. The WDA rate change affects expenditure that receives main rate WDAs, including existing pooled expenditure and new qualifying expenditure. The FYA rate change applies only to new main rate expenditure, including plant or machinery for leasing but excludes second-hand assets and cars.

(24) The behavioural response reflects some banking companies shifting profits outside of the UK in response to the higher tax rate and includes the impact of this profit shifting on both corporation tax and the bank surcharge.

(25) This measure also increases annually managed expenditure by £0.8 billion in 2027-28 as firms eligible for BICS and the Supercharger uplift receive backdated support relating to 2026-27. This increases the total cost of the scheme to £0.5 billion a year for 2027-28 onwards.

(26) This net annually managed expenditure flowing into local authorities captures retained rates and safety net payments, offset by top-up and tariffs, and levy payments.

(27) For example, the full range of relevant regulatory changes affecting any specific sector can be difficult to identify and quantify, making it difficult to form a baseline of regulation against new policy is assessed. See paragraph 4.13 of our “Briefing paper 10: Accounting for the supply-side effects of policy”.

(28) See paragraph 4.12 of our “Briefing paper 10: Accounting for the supply-side effects of policy”.

(29) Health Foundation Report 2024. The Health Foundation figures are for the extra costs of healthcare over and above current spending in 2035-36 and cover a range of different reform options. For example, the low figure estimates only cover some costs for those recipients living at home. The higher figures correspond to wider schemes e.g. including the costs of residential care.

(30) As described in paragraph 3.19, this includes the four-month freeze announced in May, and the further six-month freeze announced at this Budget.

(31) For example, changes to mortgage interest relief, the stamp duty land tax surcharge and capital gains tax allowances, and the introduction of the Renters’ Rights Act.

(32) See Department for Business and Trade, Impact assessment of the Free Trade Agreement between the UK and India, 2025. The agreement came into effect in 15 July 2026.

(33) This is because in our current medium-term forecast an uprating of 2.5 per cent would apply under both the previous and adjusted version of the triple lock in both 2030-31 and 2031-32.

(34) Average earnings growth exceeded CPI or 2.5 per cent in all years between 1990 and 2007 apart from 1995 when CPI was 0.1 percentage point above average earnings growth.

(35) This provisional estimate assumes that the average percentage difference between pension values under earnings uprating and the adjusted triple lock observed over 1990 to 2025 persists over the next 50 years. It applies this average uplift to our long-term earnings-uprated state pension projection and compounds savings as a proportion of GDP each year to produce an illustrative estimate of the resulting effect on debt. These resulting debt impact estimates are not directly comparable with those produced as part of our FRS reports which have some methodological and data input differences.

(36) National Accounts taxes include all taxes recognised in the ONS’s National Accounts and National Insurance Contributions. Public sector current receipts further include public sector interest and dividend receipts (driven by the interest received from funded public sector pensions and the accrued interest on student loans), public sector gross operating surplus and a small amount of other non-tax receipts.

(37) We take the median of the absolute level differences at each forecast horizon between outturns and the central forecasts. We use data from all spring forecasts since 2011 and all autumn forecasts since 2013. The median value is used as it is less skewed by shocks such as the Covid pandemic. These differences broadly increase each year over the forecast period. The median absolute level difference in the final year is 6.4 per cent for nominal earnings, 5.9 per cent for nominal consumption, and 10.3 per cent for equity prices.

(38) The tax gap is the difference between the amount of tax that should, in theory, be paid to HMRC (‘theoretical tax liability’), and the amount that is actually paid. See Box 3.1 in Chapter 3 for more detail. 2024-25 is the latest year for which HMRC publishes estimates, see HMRC, Measuring tax gaps 2026 edition: tax gap estimates for 2024 to 2025, June 2026.

(39) These indicative scenarios assume that the nominal GDP denominator is unaffected by the changes to the economic determinants that drive changes in receipts.

(40) Nominal earnings are the main driver of income tax and NICs receipts, with frozen tax thresholds until April 2031 amplifying the impact of nominal earnings growth on receipts (paragraph 4.14). Higher-than-expected inflation means that, despite stronger nominal earnings, real earnings are lower than our March forecast in 2026-27, as discussed in paragraph 2.29.

(41) Estimates are based on our standard ready reckoners consistent with our pre-measures forecast. They solely capture the direct effect of a 1 percentage point increase in nominal earnings and CPI on income tax and NICs receipts, including on self-assessed income tax. They exclude any wider effects on other receipts, spending, or the broader economy.

(42) See Box 3.2 in the March 2024 Economic and fiscal outlook for details on our estimation approach. We used a similar approach in the 2026 Fiscal risks and sustainability report.

(43) The NSTA’s oil and gas projections are based on the informed judgement, which is guided by survey data, comparisons with other forecasts (including forecasts by the International Energy Agency, Wood Mackenzie and National Grid Gas) and assumptions around factors which could impact the output from fields. More information is available from the Data and Insights section of the North Sea Transition Authority website.

(44) This also includes the impact of recent analysis of 2025-26 SA receipts which suggests that CGT receipts were £2.5 billion stronger than assumed at the March forecast, some of which was offset by a reallocation which reduced SA IT receipts by £1.8 billion.

(45) HMRC extracted asset-level information from taxpayer returns and constructed a counterfactual path of asset disposals across the tax year using 2023-24 data. This was compared to the actual level of asset disposals in 2024-25 to estimate the impact of forestalling.

(46) The average absolute forecast difference is calculated by taking the average of the absolute values of differences between outturns and the central (point) forecasts, ignoring whether the differences are positive or negative. This indicates the absolute size of forecast differences and therefore is a measure of how accurate the forecasts are.

(47) More formally, unless otherwise stated these terms refer, respectively, to public sector current expenditure (PSCE) in RDEL and public sector gross investment (PSGI) in CDEL, which is the spending within DELs that is recorded within the National Accounts measure of total managed expenditure.

(48) The Superannuation Contributions Adjusted for Past Experience (SCAPE) rate is the discount rate used by unfunded public service pension schemes to place a current value on expected future pension payments. HMT increased the SCAPE rate from CPI+1.7% per year to CPI+2.0% per year in May 2026.

(49) The 2024 valuation is not entirely fiscally neutral because some employers participate in public service pension schemes whose staff costs are not funded through RDEL (for example, teachers at independent schools or at some universities which use the public service Teachers’ Pension Scheme). This means there is an increase in net pension spending with no offsetting RDEL reduction. This results in an average £1.1 billion increase in spending between 2027-28 and 2031-32.

(50) Central government subsidies to public corporations – mainly payments to train-operating-companies – are now classified as part of fiscal DEL rather than AME. This was not captured in our March 2026 AME forecast and so adjusting for this increases TME by around £1.5 billion a year.

(51) The government have moved business rate under-indexation compensation and relief grants from AME to DEL. This was not fully captured in our March 2026 AME forecast and so adjusting for this decreases TME by around £0.2 billion a year.

(52) The government has moved spending on British Business Bank expected guarantees and write-offs from DEL to AME. This was captured in our March 2026 forecast and so has no effect on TME.

(53) Between April and July, departments spent 28 per cent of their total forecasted PSGI in CDEL for 2026-27. The average is around 26 per cent.

(54) We had based our previous capital underspend judgements on an assessment of previous periods of fast real growth in capital spending when departments typically underspent plans by a significant amount. This is discussed further in the Forecast evaluation reports 2025 (in paragraph 3.26) and 2026 (in paragraph 3.28), and in paragraph 5.20 in the November 2025 EFO.

(55) HM Treasury, Departmental Efficiency Plans. June 2025

(56) See NAO, The Efficiency Programme: A second review of progress, February 2007 and NAO, Efficiency and reform in government corporate functions through shared service centres, March 2012. In October 2026, the Government published 2025-26 Departmental efficiency and savings performance which reports £16 billion of efficiencies and savings in 2025-26. This does not split out cash-releasing and non-cash-releasing savings and so the implications for future spending plans and pressures are not clear. A more detailed assessment will be published by large departments in their 2026-27 Annual Reports and Accounts.

(57) Department for Education, Education, health and care plans. September 2026

(58) Between 2019 and 2026 primary schools in England reduced the number of teachers and classes, but not schools, in response to falling primary school pupils. The outlook on school funding in England, Institute for Fiscal Studies. September 2026

(59) Immigration Service Statistics, Home Office. June 2026

(60) NAO, Building an effective and resilient probation service, October 2025, the NAO estimates that 3,150 extra probation service staff undertaking sentence management are needed in 2026-27.

(61) Criminal court statistics quarterly: April to June 2026, all open cases in the crown court in England and Wales in 2025-26 are estimated at 79,706.

(62) Tribunal statistics quarterly, September 2026. 2025-26 has an open caseload of 860,491 compared to 898,100 in 2012-13.

(63) Calculation is based on Spending Reviews between 2000 and 2021, excluding pandemic affected Spending Reviews in 2019 and 2020.

(64) MHCLG, The Social and Affordable Homes Programme and the reinvigoration of council housebuilding. August 2026

(65) MHCLG, Launching the Social and Affordable Homes Programme. November 2025

(66) We assume that half of the 180,000 social homes will be built by councils and the rest built by housing associations.

(67) 2035-36 estimates by the Health Foundation, Social care funding reform in England. June 2026

(68) Incapacity caseloads here refer to claimants in Great Britain on either employment and support allowance, the universal credit health journey, or both. Disability caseloads refer to claimants in England and Wales on disability living allowance, personal independence payment, or attendance allowance.

(69) The forecast assumes CPI uprating of 3.1 per cent in 2027-28. After our forecast was finalised, CPI inflation outturn for September 2026 was published which, if incorporated into our forecast, would have increased CPI uprating for 2027-28 to 3.5 per cent. This would have increased welfare spending by an average of £0.9 billion from 2027-28 onwards.

(70) See paragraphs 3.10 and 3.11 in our 2026 Welfare Trends Report.

(71) EFS is a mechanism offered by central government to local authorities that would otherwise be at risk of being unable to meet their statutory requirement to balance their budget. It is primarily provided through capitalisation directions, which allow local authorities to use capital resource, including borrowing and capital receipts, to fund spending. A smaller proportion is typically delivered through additional council tax flexibilities.

(72) A survey of 89 local authorities found here: Society of County Treasurers and Association of Local Authority Treasurer Societies and County Councils Network., Overview of the 2026 SEND Deficits Survey, October 2026.

(73) The one-time transfer was previously estimated to be valued at £6 billion in 2026-27 and is neutral for PSNB but does have implications for the central government net cash requirement as discussed in paragraph 5.17 of our March 2026 EFO.

(74) DfE, Children looked after in England including adoptions, 26 November 2025

(75) The effective interest rate is central government interest payments, net of the APF, as a share of central government gross debt.

(76) From February 2030, the ONS RPI measure will be aligned to CPIH. See Box 2.3 of our October 2024 EFO for a more detailed explanation of this change.

(77) The Post Office redress schemes refer to the following redress schemes: Horizon Shortfall Scheme, HSS Appeals, Overturned Convictions, Horizon Convictions Redress Scheme, Group Litigation Order, Capture Redress Scheme, Family Members Redress Scheme.

(78) These illiquid financial assets acquired by the PuFins are not counted within public sector net debt, which therefore only records the gilts issued to finance their acquisition. But the assets are included within public sector net financial liabilities, which are described from paragraph 6.27. The interest payable on the gilts issued to finance the activities of the PuFins is captured within our forecast for central government debt interest spending, the income on the assets is mostly recorded within the interest receipts forecast, and the losses on the assets are recorded as capital spending. For more information on how PuFins affect the public finances and the associated risks and uncertainties see Lockwood, I., M. Hanson and T. Baiju, Forecasting public financial institutions, OBR articles, August 2026. UK Government Investments produces experimental estimates of the performance of the assets of PuFins relative to financing costs in its Financial Investment Report 2026.

(79) Bank of England, Monetary Policy Summary and Minutes, September 2026. The Bank has stated that it will retain £120 billion of the longest-dated gilts held within the APF. Of the remaining £368 billion, it has stated that £222 billion will unwind passively and £146 billion will be sold following the MPC’s intended annual sales pace of £20 billion starting in April 2027. This means the APF will not be fully unwound until 2071.

(80) A full estimation of the APF’s impact on the public finances requires consideration of central government financing. Interest losses and valuation losses impact the financing requirement, which is largely met through gilt sales.

(81) The lifetime cost on a net present value basis is discounted by weighted-average gilt yields. The real terms estimate is deflated by CPI. We have used these approaches given the extended lifetime of the APF following the Bank’s announcements in September. These estimates are therefore not directly comparable with the cash estimates produced in previous EFOs.

(82) The Bank of England’s, Asset Purchase Facility Quarter 2 2026 report notes that the difference in the pace of unwind across various scenarios have a minimal impact on the respective cumulative lifetime net present value.

(83) Figures, in net present value terms, from Bank of England, Asset Purchase Facility Quarterly Report, Quarter 3 2025.

(84) The forecast assumes that the active sales of gilts held within the APF are made directly to the private sector. The Bank of England and HM Treasury are considering a new model from April 2027, under which the DMO would instead purchase gilts sold by the APF, with these gilts subsequently cancelled and the purchases financed by a corresponding amount issued by the DMO. If implemented, this would shift the associated increase in private sector gilt holdings from APF sales to gross gilt issuance, while keeping overall private sector holdings unchanged. A final decision on whether to proceed with this change has not been taken and so it is not reflected in our forecast. See HM Treasury, Update on the Asset Purchase Facility (APF), September 2026.

(85) See IMF, World economic outlook, October 2026. The IMF fiscal projection is based on our March 2026 forecast, adjusted for differences in assumptions.

(86) The probability distributions for the current budget deficit are based on stochastic simulations, which generate thousands of potential outcomes whose chances of occurring are based on randomly selected shocks drawn historical outturn. We use historical deviations from previous forecasts to generate the probability distributions for PSNFL. We only began forecasting PSNFL in 2016 so there would be a limited sample from which to generate stochastic simulations, and from a period which has seen relatively high variation in borrowing and debt. More detail can be found in the October 2024 EFO and in Steel, D., OBR Working Paper No. 17: Evaluating forecast uncertainty with stochastic simulations, December 2021.

(87) Underlying forecast changes include the effects of new outturn data, changes in the economic forecast, revisions to forecasting judgements and assumptions, and methodological changes. It excludes the effects of new policies announced since our March 2026 forecast.

(88) The supplementary target requires PSNFL as a share of GDP to fall between 2028-29 and 2029-30. Higher nominal GDP growth in 2029-30 will, other things equal, reduce PSNFL as a share of GDP in that year and therefore increase the degree by which it falls in that year.

(89) This absolute average is based on forecasts since October 2021. This calculation is made on a borrowing basis.

(90) This absolute median is based on all OBR forecasts exclusive of the current forecast. This calculation is made on a borrowing basis.

(91) See Health Foundation, Social care funding reform in England, June 2025. These figures are for the extra costs of healthcare over and above current spending.

(92) Sensitivities are estimated using elasticities (for nominal GDP growth and the effective tax rate sensitivities) and our debt interest ready reckoner (for the Bank Rate and gilt yield and inflation sensitivities). See our ready reckoner available on our website.

(93) From February 2030, the ONS RPI measure will be aligned to CPIH.

(94) In these scenarios, we model the Bank Rate adjustment using the “contemporaneous Taylor-type rule (headline inflation)” from the Bank of England April MPR, and assume this converges to the Bank Rate path in our central forecast in the medium term. In practice, changes to Bank Rate are based on the collective judgement of the Monetary Policy Committee. We model the adjustment in gilt yields assuming a one percentage point increase in the risk premium alongside an estimate of the present value of changes in the Bank Rate, and use the 10-year mark as a proxy for the curve given the average maturity of UK public debt.

(95) We do not make an explicit adjustment for the measure to temporarily remove VAT on domestic electricity bills. But we do not believe this would substantially change the results.

(96) This is estimated by uprating departmental spending with the additional growth in the GDP deflator in the second-round effects scenario relative to our central forecast. Absent second-round effects, additional GDP deflator growth is around three-quarters lower in the medium term. As this is illustrative, we do not account for the impacts of higher departmental spending on debt interest spending, or on the wider economy.

(97) We do not model additional disinflationary second-round effects from a faster recovery of oil and gas prices, which we would expect to be modest. We also do not illustrate an implied easing of DEL pressure, as the changes in the GDP deflator in this scenario are relatively small.

(98) Like in the severe shock scenario, this is based on the “contemporaneous Taylor-type rule (headline inflation)” from the Bank of England’s April MPR, which we then assume converges to the Bank Rate path in our central forecast in the medium term.

This website uses cookies to improve your experience while you navigate through the website. Out of these, the cookies that are categorized as necessary are stored on your browser as they are essential for the working of basic functionalities of the website. We also use third-party cookies that help us analyze and understand how you use this website. These cookies will be stored in your browser only with your consent. You also have the option to opt-out of these cookies. But opting out of some of these cookies may affect your browsing experience.
Necessary
Always Enabled
Necessary cookies are absolutely essential for the website to function properly. These cookies ensure basic functionalities and security features of the website, anonymously.
CookieDurationDescription
JSESSIONIDsessionUsed by sites written in JSP. General purpose platform session cookies that are used to maintain users' state across page requests.
viewed_cookie_policy1 yearThe cookie is set by the GDPR Cookie Consent plugin and is used to store whether or not user has consented to the use of cookies. It does not store any personal data.
__cfduid1 monthThe cookie is used by cdn services like CloudFare to identify individual clients behind a shared IP address and apply security settings on a per-client basis. It does not correspond to any user ID in the web application and does not store any personally identifiable information.

Analytics
Analytical cookies are used to understand how visitors interact with the website. These cookies help provide information on metrics the number of visitors, bounce rate, traffic source, etc.
CookieDurationDescription
_ga2 yearsThis cookie is installed by Google Analytics. The cookie is used to calculate visitor, session, campaign data and keep track of site usage for the site's analytics report. The cookies store information anonymously and assign a randomly generated number to identify unique visitors.
_gid1 dayThis cookie is installed by Google Analytics. The cookie is used to store information of how visitors use a website and helps in creating an analytics report of how the website is doing. The data collected including the number visitors, the source where they have come from, and the pages visted in an anonymous form.
Performance
Performance cookies are used to understand and analyze the key performance indexes of the website which helps in delivering a better user experience for the visitors.
CookieDurationDescription
YSCsessionThis cookies is set by Youtube and is used to track the views of embedded videos.
__utma2 yearsThis cookie is set by Google Analytics and is used to distinguish users and sessions. The cookie is created when the JavaScript library executes and there are no existing __utma cookies. The cookie is updated every time data is sent to Google Analytics.
__utmb30 minutesThe cookie is set by Google Analytics. The cookie is used to determine new sessions/visits. The cookie is created when the JavaScript library executes and there are no existing __utma cookies. The cookie is updated every time data is sent to Google Analytics.
__utmcsessionThe cookie is set by Google Analytics and is deleted when the user closes the browser. The cookie is not used by ga.js. The cookie is used to enable interoperability with urchin.js which is an older version of Google analytics and used in conjunction with the __utmb cookie to determine new sessions/visits.
__utmt10 minutesThe cookie is set by Google Analytics and is used to throttle request rate.
__utmz6 monthsThis cookie is set by Google analytics and is used to store the traffic source or campaign through which the visitor reached your site.
